**What happened:** When أبو بكر ؓ died, عمر ؓ called the trustees together and had the treasury
opened. They went through it. There was one dinar in it, fallen out of a sack. They asked God's
mercy on him.
**The statement:**
> وَلَمَّا تُوُفِّيَ أَبُو بَكْرٍ جَمَعَ عُمَرُ الْأُمَنَاءِ وَفَتَحَ بَيْتَ الْمَالِ، فَلَمْ يَجِدُوا فِيهِ شَيْئًا غَيْرَ دِينَارٍ سَقَطَ مِنْ غِرَارَةٍ، فَتَرَحَّمُوا عَلَيْهِ.
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۵ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/956
> *English:* "When Abū Bakr died, ʿUmar gathered the trustees and opened the treasury. They found
> nothing in it but a single dinar that had fallen out of a sack. And they asked God's mercy on him."
**عبرت:** He did not build a reserve; he spent it on the people it belonged to, and left the shelf
bare.
**Hands-up?** no
> **Delivery note:** say *"ابن الاثیر records that…"*. This is a transmitted report, not an audit.

### ABD/E-B13 · The daughter's verse, and the father's correction
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** the last hours `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** nothing moves.
**What happened:** عائشة ؓ was with him at the end. A faintness took him. She said a line of poetry
aloud — the old Arab line about tears that must one day be poured out. He lifted his head and stopped
her. *Little daughter, it is not like that.* And he gave her the verse instead: **"and the agony of
death comes with the truth — that is what you were shying away from."**
**The statement:**
> فرفع راسه وقال: يا بنية ليس كذاك، ولكن كما قال الله تعالى: {وَجَاءَتْ سَكْرَةُ الْمَوْتِ بِالْحَقِّ ذَلِكَ مَا كُنْتَ مِنْهُ تَحِيدُ} [ق: ١٩]
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1166
> *English:* "He lifted his head and said: little daughter, it is not so; rather it is as God said —
> 'and the agony of death comes with the truth: that is what you were shying away from.'"
**عبرت:** In his last hour he was still correcting a beautiful sentence into a true one.
**Hands-up?** no
> The Arabic of the line she recited, if it is wanted — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۸ ·
> https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1166, and الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۵ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/956:
>
> > لعمرك ما يغني الثراء عن الفتى ... إذا حشرجت يوما وضاق بها الصدر
> ⚠ «راسه» in the Shamela text is a slip for «رأسه».

### ABD/E-B14 · The shroud
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** the last hours `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** nothing moves.
**What happened:** He gave instructions for his own washing — his wife أسماء بنت عميس ؓ, and his son
عبد الرحمن ؓ. Then the shroud. Use the two garments I am wearing, and buy one more to go with them.
And he said: the living need the new more than the dead.
**The statement:**
> وَقَالَ: الْحَيُّ أَحْوَجُ إِلَى الْجَدِيدِ مِنَ الْمَيِّتِ، إِنَّمَا هُوَ لِلْمُهْلَةِ وَالصَّدِيدِ.
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/953
> *English:* "He said: the living have more need of the new than the dead — this is only for the
> decay."
**عبرت:** The last instruction he gave about himself was an instruction to spend less on himself.
**Hands-up?** no

### ABD/E-B15 · Monday night, and a grave already dug beside another
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** Monday evening, 8 nights remaining of جمادى الآخرة 13 AH `[SOURCED]` ·
**Map:** the pin on المدینہ. Then, on the diagram of the three graves: three positions, one behind
the other.
**What happened:** He died on the Monday evening. He had asked to be buried beside the
Messenger of God ﷺ, so they dug there. عمر ؓ prayed over him in the Prophet's ﷺ mosque, four takbīrs.
They carried him on the same bier the Prophet ﷺ had been carried on. Four men went down into the
grave: his son عبد الرحمن ؓ, عمر ؓ, عثمان ؓ, and طلحة ؓ. They laid his head at the Prophet's ﷺ
shoulders and put his niche against the Prophet's ﷺ niche. He was buried before morning came. He was
sixty-three — the age at which the Prophet ﷺ had died.
**The statement:**
> وقالت عائشة: مات ليلة الثلاثاء، ودفن قبل أن يصبح.
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1167
> *English:* "ʿĀʾisha ؓ said: he died on the night of Tuesday, and was buried before morning came."
**عبرت:** Two years and three months of authority, and it ended in a room, at night, with four men
and a spade.
**Hands-up?** no
> For the diagram caption, one sentence fixes all three graves — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۹ ·
> https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1167:
>
> > رأس أبي بكر عند كتفي رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم، ورأس عمر عند حقوي أبي بكر.
> **Do not claim the burial was "in عائشة ؓ's house" from these pages** — they say *beside the
> Prophet ﷺ*, and no more.

### ABD/E-B16 · علي ؓ speaks over the body
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** before the burial `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** nothing moves.
**What happened:** The body had been laid out and covered. علي ؓ came in, looked at it, and said one
sentence: there is nobody whose record I would rather meet God carrying than this man lying here.
**The statement:**
> دخل علي على أبي بكر بعد ما سجي، فقال: ما أحد ألقى الله بصحيفته أحب إلي من هذا المسجي.
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1167
> *English:* "ʿAlī came in to Abū Bakr after he had been laid out, and said: there is no one whose
> record I would rather meet God with than this man lying here."
**عبرت:** The men who were there are the best witnesses to each other, and this is one of them
speaking.
**Hands-up?** no
> **Delivery note:** name the transmitter — الذہبی رحمہ اللہ carries it **from أبو جعفر الباقر
> رحمہ اللہ**. Say that once, plainly, and move on. Do not editorialise; the sentence does its own work.

### ABD/E-B17 · The last words
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the last moment `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** nothing moves.
**What happened:** The last thing he said was not about the state, or the armies, or the succession
he had spent his final days arranging. It was four words of duʿāʾ.
**The statement:**
> وَكَانَ آخِرُ مَا تَكَلَّمَ بِهِ: تَوَفَّنِي مُسْلِمًا وَأَلْحِقْنِي بِالصَّالِحِينَ.
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/954
> *English:* "The last thing he spoke was: take me as a Muslim, and join me to the righteous."
**عبرت:** He asked, at the end, for the only thing he had ever actually been working for.
**Hands-up?** no
> **Attribution caution:** found **only** in الکامل among the pages read. Say *"ابن الاثیر records
> that his last words were…"*, not *"it is narrated"*.

### ABD/E-B18 · The news reaches اليرموك
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** 13 AH, at the Syrian front `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** this is the card where
the map moves — the pin jumps from المدینہ to the اليرموك valley, and the two arrows the whole session
has been drawing finally meet the news.
**What happened:** Letters went out to the commanders in الشام carrying the news, with شدّاد بن أوس ؓ
and مَحْميَة بن جَزْء ؓ. The messengers arrived while the army was drawn up facing the Byzantines at
اليرموك. Afterwards, standing among the captured tents and the spoils, خالد ؓ told the men. ابن کثیر
records what that morning was like in one sentence.
**The statement:**
> وما فرحوا بما وجدوا بقدر حزنهم على الصدِّيق حين أعلمهم خالد بذلك، ولكن عوضهم الله بالفاروق ﵁.
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3243
> *English:* "Their joy at what they found was not equal to their grief for the Ṣiddīq when Khālid
> told them of it — but God gave them the Fārūq ؓ in exchange."
**عبرت:** The work outlived the man, and that is the whole test of whether the work was ever his.
**Hands-up?** no
> The dispatch itself — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۰۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3248:
>
> > وقد كتب بوفاة الصدِّيق إلى أمراء الشام مع شدَّاد بن أوس، ومَحْميَة بن جَزْء (٥)، فوصلا والناس مصافون جيوش الروم يوم اليرموك كما قدمنا.

### ABD/E-B19 · عمر ؓ takes the minbar
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the day of the burial `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** المدینہ.
**What happened:** Once أبو بكر ؓ was in the ground, عمر ؓ went up and addressed the people. He did
not begin with condolences. He began with a picture of what he thought he had taken charge of — a
camel with a ring through its nose, which will follow whoever holds the rope, so the man holding the
rope had better look where he is going. And then: *as for me, by the Lord of the Kaʿba, I will carry
you on the road.*
**The statement:**
> إِنَّمَا مَثَلُ الْعَرَبِ مَثَلُ جَمَلٍ أَنِفٍ اتَّبَعَ قَائِدَهُ، فَلْيَنْظُرْ قَائِدُهُ حَيْثُ يَقُودُهُ، وَأَمَّا أَنَا فَوَرَبِّ الْكَعْبَةِ لَأَحْمِلَنَّكُمْ عَلَى الطَّرِيقِ!
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/959
> *English:* "The Arabs are only like a nose-ringed camel that follows its driver — so let its driver
> look where he is leading it. As for me, by the Lord of the Kaʿba, I shall carry you on the road."
**عبرت:** The second man did not promise to be gentle; he promised to keep them on the road.
**Hands-up?** no

### ABD/E-B20 · The وصية to عمر ؓ — the two rights and the scales
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** after the covenant was read `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** nothing moves.
**What happened:** He had عمر ؓ brought in and told him he had appointed him over the Companions of
the Messenger of God ﷺ. Then he gave him a piece of advice that had nothing to do with governing.
God has a right by night He will not accept by day, and a right by day He will not accept by night;
and He accepts nothing voluntary until the obligation is discharged. Then he took him through the
scales of the Day of Judgement, and closed: keep this, and let nothing absent be dearer to you than
the one present thing — death — and you will not escape it.
**The statement:**
> يَا عُمَرُ، إِنَّ لِلَّهِ حَقًّا بِاللَّيْلِ لَا يَقْبَلُهُ فِي النَّهَارِ، وَحَقًّا فِي النَّهَارِ لَا يَقْبَلُهُ بِاللَّيْلِ، وَإِنَّهُ لَا يَقْبَلُ نَافِلَةً حَتَّى تُؤَدَّى الْفَرِيضَةُ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/958
> *English:* "ʿUmar — God has a right by night that He does not accept by day, and a right by day
> that He does not accept by night; and He accepts no voluntary act until the obligation has been
> discharged."
**عبرت:** The handover instruction to the most powerful man alive was about the five prayers.
**Hands-up?** no
> The وصية spans ج۲ ص۲۶۷–۲۶۸; the closing line is at ص۲۶۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/959.

### ABD/E-B21 · His father outlives him
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** 13 AH, and six months after `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** nothing moves.
**⚠ Not established:** none of the pages fetched in this pass says where أبو قحافة ؓ was living when
his son died. **Do not put a second pin on مكة** without a citation.
**What happened:** أبو بكر ؓ was the first caliph to hold the office while his own father was still
alive. When he died, his father inherited a share of him — and returned it to his son's children.
أبو قحافة ؓ lived on for six months and some days, and died at ninety-seven.
**The statement:**
> وعن مجاهد قال: كلم أبو قحافة في ميراثه من ابنه، فقال: قد رددت ذلك على ولده، ثم لم يعش بعده إلا ستة أشهر وأياما.
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1167
> *English:* "Mujāhid said: Abū Quḥāfa was spoken to about his inheritance from his son, and he said:
> I have returned that to his children. Then he lived only six months and some days after him."
**عبرت:** Nobody in that family took what they could have taken.
**Hands-up?** no
> Corroboration, both in الکامل:
>
> > وَعَاشَ أَبُوهُ بَعْدَهُ سِتَّةَ أَشْهُرٍ وَأَيَّامًا، وَمَاتَ وَلَهُ سَبْعٌ وَتِسْعُونَ سَنَةً.
> > — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/955
>
> > وَكَانَ أَوَّلَ وَالٍ فَرَضَ لَهُ رَعِيَّتُهُ نَفَقَتَهُ، وَأَوَّلَ خَلِيفَةٍ وَلِيَ وَأَبُوهُ حَيٌّ، وَأَوَّلَ مَنْ سَمَّى مُصْحَفَ الْقُرْآنِ مُصْحَفًا، وَأَوَّلَ مَنْ سُمِّيَ خَلِيفَةً.
> > — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/957

### ABD/E-B22 · He kept milking their goats
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the first six months of the caliphate `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** السُّنْح, the
outlying quarter — then the move into المدینہ proper.
**What happened:** For the first six months after the بیعت he went on living at السُّنْح with his wife
and walking into المدینہ on foot to lead the prayer, and he went on going to the market to buy and
sell. He also used to milk the neighbourhood's sheep for them. When the بیعت happened a girl of the
quarter said out loud: well, he won't be milking our sheep for us now. He heard her, and answered her
directly. Six months later he gave up trade — *a people's affairs are not put right alongside a
business* — and moved into the city.
**The statement:**
> فَسَمِعَهَا فَقَالَ: بَلَى لَعَمْرِي لَأَحْلِبَنَّهَا لَكُمْ، وَإِنِّي لَأَرْجُو أَنْ لَا يُغَيِّرَ بِي مَا دَخَلْتُ فِيهِ. فَكَانَ يَحْلِبُ لَهُمْ.
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/957
> *English:* "He heard her and said: on the contrary — by my life, I shall milk them for you; and I
> hope that what I have entered into will not change me. And he went on milking for them."
**عبرت:** He said out loud, on the first week, the thing office usually takes from a man — and then he
proved it for two years.
**Hands-up?** Before telling it: *"Hands up — think of someone who got a promotion and stayed exactly
the same person."*
> The reason he gave for finally leaving trade, on the same page — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۶ ·
> https://shamela.ws/book/21712/957:
>
> > مَا تَصْلُحُ أُمُورُ النَّاسِ مَعَ التِّجَارَةِ، وَمَا يَصْلُحُ إِلَّا التَّفَرُّغُ لَهُمْ وَالنَّظَرُ فِي شَأْنِهِمْ.

### ABD/E-B23 · The illness, second account — the poison
**Tier:** CUT · **When:** a year before the death, on this account `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none.
**What happened:** Our sources also carry a second account of the cause. الزهري relates that
أبو بكر ؓ and الحارث بن كلدة were eating a dish sent to him, and الحارث said: lift your hand, Caliph
of the Messenger of God — there is a year's poison in this, and you and I will die on the same day.
They both remained ill and died on one day at the end of the year. سیر elsewhere gives it flatly as
«يقال», and الکامل gives both this and the cold-bath account without choosing.
**The statement:**
> فقال الحارث: ارفع يدك يا خليفة رسول الله، والله إن فيها لسم سنة، وأنا وأنت نموت في يوم واحد
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۵–۱۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1163
> *English:* "al-Ḥārith said: lift your hand, Caliph of the Messenger of God — by God, there is a
> year's poison in this, and you and I will die on one day."
**عبرت:** — (none offered; the card is recorded, not delivered.)
**Hands-up?** no
> **Why CUT.** It sits awkwardly beside عائشة ؓ's fifteen-day account, it is given as «يقال», and the
> bare version names a community as the agent. **Not for the hall.** Recorded so it is never
> re-researched, and logged for `QA_BANK.md` as a floor question.
> Also at الکامل ج۲ ص۲۶۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/953 and سیر الراشدون ص۲۰ ·
> https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1168.

### ABD/E-B24 · The three regrets
**Tier:** CUT · **When:** the last days `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none.
**What happened:** الذہبی carries, through صالح بن كيسان, a long deathbed report in which أبو بكر ؓ
lists three things he wishes he had not done, three he wishes he had done, and three he wishes he had
asked the Prophet ﷺ. The list is at سیر الراشدون ص۱۷–۱۸.
**The statement:** none in the sources we use — **for delivery purposes.** The Arabic is on the page
at https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1165 and https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1166, and is deliberately not
reproduced on a card that could be read aloud by mistake.
**عبرت:** — (none offered.)
**Hands-up?** no
> **Why CUT.** The first item in the list touches the مشاجرات directly. `CLAUDE.md` §1.6 forbids
> improvising on that from the floor, and this session is not the place where it is handled with
> اصول. **The one separable, safe sentence from that same report** is usable on its own and is
> quoted in full at §9.1 — سیر أعلام النبلاء، ج الراشدون ص۱۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1165:
>
> > جعلت لكم عهدا بعدي، واخترت لكم خيركم في نفسي فكلكم ورم لذلك أنفه رجاء أن يكون الأمر له.
> and is quoted in full at §9.1.

---

## Iraq, Shām, and يرموك

*Source note: [`iraq-syria-and-yarmuk-12-15ah.md`](../docs/research/iraq-syria-and-yarmuk-12-15ah.md) — read it for the pages behind these cards.*

### ISA/E-C1 · The order to Iraq
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** المحرم 12 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۶۲]` · **Map:** first arrow leaves
Arabia — from اليمامة east to the lower Euphrates. Arabia stops being the whole board.
**What happened:** Khālid ؓ had just finished al-Yamāma. A letter came from Abū Bakr ؓ in Medina. It
did not say "conquer Iraq." It said: come at Iraq from its lower end. Call the people to God. If
they answer, they are Muslims — what is yours is theirs. If they refuse, take the jizya. If they
refuse that, fight. And then two restrictions that are easy to miss. Do not compel any man to march
with you. And do not use any man who apostatised, even if he has come back.
**The statement:**
> وأن يَتألَّفَ الناسَ ويدعوهم إلى الله ﷿، فإن أجابوا وإلا أخذ منهم الجزيةَ فإن امتنعوا عن ذلك قاتلهم،
> وأمره أن لا يُكْرِه أحدًا على المسير معه، ولا يستعينُ بمنِ ارتدّ عن الإسلام وإن كان [قد] عاد إليه
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۶۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3208
> *English:* "…that he should win the people over and call them to God; if they answered, well, and
> if not, take the jizya from them; and if they refused that, fight them. And he ordered him not to
> compel anyone to march with him, and not to make use of any man who had apostatised from Islam,
> even if he had returned to it."
**عبرت:** A state that has just won a civil war and still refuses to conscript the defeated is
telling you what it thinks it is for.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C2 · The letter to the marzubāns of Persia
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** 12 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۶۴]` · **Map:** a line drawn from الحيرة to
المدائن — the first time Medina addresses the Persian court directly.
**What happened:** Khālid ؓ had taken al-Ḥīra. Upriver from it stood al-Madāʾin — the throne of
Persia, the iwān of Chosroes. He did not march on it. He wrote to it. The letter was short and it
survives, because al-Shaʿbī says the family of Buqayla in al-Ḥīra read it out to him. It sets out
one offer, one alternative, and one warning. Ibn Kathīr رحمہ اللہ ends the page on their reaction:
when they read it, they were left marvelling.
**The statement:**
> فابعثوا إليَّ بالرُّهن واعتقدوا مني الذِّمة، و (إلا) فوالذي لا إله غيره لأبعثن إليكم قومًا يحبّون الموتَ
> كما تحبّون أنتم الحياة
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۶۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3210
> *English:* "…so send me hostages and take a covenant of protection from me. Otherwise — by Him
> beside whom there is no god — I shall send against you a people who love death as you love life."
**عبرت:** A man who has made peace with his own death is very difficult to bargain with.
**Hands-up?** Ask the room: how far is Medina from the Persian capital? Then tell them a letter went,
before an army did.

### ISA/E-C3 · The poison cup at al-Ḥīra
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** 12 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۷۰; also سیر ج۱ ص۳۷۶, where the chain is
مرسل]` · **Map:** al-Ḥīra pinned — the old Arab-Christian capital of the Lakhmids, now negotiating.
**What happened:** The man who negotiated for al-Ḥīra was ʿAmr b. ʿAbd al-Masīḥ b. Buqayla, a
Christian Arab, very old. Khālid ؓ noticed a pouch on him and asked what was in it. Poison, the man
said — enough to kill within the hour. And why do you carry it? So that if I see ruin coming on my
people, I can take it. Khālid ؓ took the pouch out of his hand, said the name of God over it, and —
as the commanders reached out to stop him — swallowed it. Ibn Buqayla, watching, said that he had
never seen a day of plainer good fortune than this; and he turned to the people of al-Ḥīra, who then
asked Khālid ؓ for terms. (The page does not add a sentence about the poison's effect; it moves
straight to the treaty. Narrate only that far.)
**The statement:**
> إنه لن تموت نفس حتى تأتي على أجلها … بسم الله خير الأسماء، رب الأرض والسماء، الذي ليس يضرُ مع اسمه
> داء، الرحمن الرحيم
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۷۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3216
> *English:* "No soul dies until it reaches its appointed term … In the name of God, best of names,
> Lord of earth and sky, with whose name no sickness can harm — the Merciful, the Compassionate."
**عبرت:** Certainty about the appointed hour is not recklessness; it is what makes a man usable.
**Hands-up?** no
*(Note for the speaker: al-Dhahabī رحمہ اللہ carries the same incident by a different route where the
chain is broken — the narrator did not hear it from Khālid ؓ. Tell it as a report the books carry.)*

### ISA/E-C4 · The secret ḥajj and the letter of reproof
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ذو القعدة–ذو الحجة 12 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۷۸]` · **Map:** a thin
line looping from al-Firāḍ on the Roman frontier all the way down to Mecca and back — a route
"nobody had travelled before."
**What happened:** After the fighting at al-Firāḍ, Khālid ؓ sent the army back to al-Ḥīra and let
them believe he was riding in the rear. Instead he turned south with a few companions and rode to
Mecca by a road nobody had used, performed the pilgrimage, and caught up with the rearguard before
it got home. Almost nobody knew. Abū Bakr ؓ only found out when the pilgrims came back to Medina and
mentioned it. He wrote to him. The punishment was to be moved from the Iraq front to the Syrian one.
And with the reprimand came a warning about something other than absence.
**The statement:**
> فليهنئك أبا سليمان النية والحظوة، فأتممْ يُتمِّمِ الله لكَ، ولا يدخلنك عُجْبٌ فتخسر وتخذل، وإياكَ أن
> تُدلَّ بعمل فإنَّ اللهَ له المنُّ وهو وليّ الجزاء
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۷۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3224
> *English:* "May the intention and the favour bring you joy, Abū Sulaymān; finish, and God will
> finish for you. But let no self-admiration enter you, or you will lose and be abandoned; and
> beware of being made proud by a deed, for the bounty is God's and He is the master of reward."
**عبرت:** The reprimand a commander needs is rarely about the rule he broke.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C5 · The march across the waterless desert
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** 13 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۸۷; الکامل ج۲ ص۲۵۳; سیر ج۱ ص۳۶۶]` ·
**Map:** THE map moment of the evening. A line strikes north-west out of Iraq at Qurāqir, crosses
blank desert with no road on it, and comes out at Suwā and Tadmur — behind the Roman armies.
**What happened:** The commanders in Syria wrote to Abū Bakr ؓ asking for reinforcement and telling
him what the Romans had gathered at the Yarmūk. He wrote back to Khālid ؓ in Iraq: put a deputy over
Iraq, bring the men you have to Syria, and when you reach them you are the commander over them. The
letter reached Iraq and Khālid ؓ set off at once — but not by any road. He chose the
Samāwa, five days of waterless country. His guide was Rāfiʿ b. ʿUmayra al-Ṭāʾī, and Rāfiʿ told him
plainly it could not be done with cavalry and baggage: a lone rider fears that road. Khālid ؓ said he
had no choice; he had to come out behind the Romans. So they prepared. The strongest camels were made
thirsty, then watered twice over, their lips cut and their mouths bound so they could not chew and
lose the water. Every day they opened the bellies of ten of them and watered the horses from what was
inside. On the last stretch Rāfiʿ, whose eyes were bad, asked the men whether they could see a
box-thorn bush the height of a seated man. They said no. He said: then by God you are dead, and I am
dead with you. They looked again. The bush was there, cut down to a stump. They dug at its root and
found the spring.
**The statement:**
> وَاللَّهِ مَا وَرَدْتُ هَذَا الْمَاءَ قَطُّ إِلَّا مَرَّةً وَاحِدَةً مَعَ أَبِي وَأَنَا غُلَامٌ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۵۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/944
> *English:* "By God, I have come to this water only once in my life — with my father, when I was a
> boy."
**عبرت:** The march was survived on preparation and on one man's memory; neither was an accident.
**Hands-up?** Ask: how do you carry water for an army across five days of desert, with no wells? Take
two answers. Then tell them.
*(Length: البدایہ says nine days, الکامل and سیر say five nights. Say both.)*

### ISA/E-C6 · "At daybreak the people praise the night march"
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** 13 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۸۸]` · **Map:** the same line, arriving.
**What happened:** On that crossing an Arab of the country had told Khālid ؓ that if he reached a
certain tree by morning he and his men would live, and if he missed it they would die. They rode
through the night, hard, and came up to it at first light. Khālid ؓ said one sentence. Ibn Kathīr
رحمہ اللہ notes that it became a proverb in Arabic and that Khālid ؓ was the first man to say it.
**The statement:**
> عندَ الصَّباح يحمد القوم السُّرى
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۸۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3234
> *English:* "At daybreak the people praise the night march."
**عبرت:** The night the effort is made and the morning it is judged are never the same morning.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C7 · Four commanders, four roads
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** early 13 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۸۳–۸۴]` · **Map:** four arrows leave
Medina at once, on four separate roads, and four sectors of Syria light up — Damascus, Homs,
Palestine, Jordan.
**What happened:** Abū Bakr ؓ finished with Arabia and turned to Syria. He did not send one army. He
appointed four commanders and gave each of them a province before it was taken: Yazīd b. Abī
Sufyān ؓ Damascus, Abū ʿUbayda ؓ Homs, ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ ؓ Palestine, Shuraḥbīl b. Ḥasana ؓ Jordan. He
ordered each to take a different road. And he walked out of Medina on foot beside each of them while
they rode, giving his instructions. Ibn Kathīr رحمہ اللہ says the four roads were not a military
trick: the caliph was following a verse.
**The statement:**
> ﴿يَابَنِيَّ لَا تَدْخُلُوا مِنْ بَابٍ وَاحِدٍ وَادْخُلُوا مِنْ أَبْوَابٍ مُتَفَرِّقَةٍ﴾ [يوسف: ٦٧]
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۸۳–۸۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3229 (opening words) and
> https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3230 (the rest of the verse)
> *English:* "O my sons, do not enter by one gate; enter by separate gates." — Yūsuf 67, quoted by
> Ibn Kathīr as the precedent Abū Bakr ؓ was following.
**عبرت:** A plan is safer when it is not one plan.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C8 · The spy's report
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** أجنادين, 2 nights remaining of جمادى الأولى 13 AH `[SOURCED — الکامل
ج۲ ص۲۶۱; البدایہ ج۷ ص۸۹]` · **Map:** Ajnādayn pinned, between al-Ramla and Bayt Jibrīn in Palestine.
**What happened:** Before the battle at Ajnādayn the Roman commander wanted to know what he was
facing, so he sent a Christian Arab into the Muslim camp to look. The man stayed a day and a night
and came back. He did not report numbers or weapons. He reported a description. And the commander,
hearing it, gave the reply of a man who has understood something before his own army has.
**The statement:**
> بِاللَّيْلِ رُهْبَانٌ وَبِالنَّهَارِ فُرْسَانٌ، وَلَوْ سَرَقَ ابْنُ مَلِكِهِمْ قَطَعُوهُ، وَلَوْ زَنَى رُجِمَ … فَقَالَ: إِنْ كُنْتَ
> صَدَقْتَنِي لَبَطْنُ الْأَرْضِ خَيْرٌ مِنْ لِقَاءِ هَؤُلَاءِ عَلَى ظَهْرِهَا
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۶۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/952
> *English:* "By night monks, by day horsemen; if their king's son stole they would cut off his
> hand, and if he committed adultery he would be stoned." He said: "If you have told me the truth,
> then the belly of the earth is better than meeting these men on its back."
**عبرت:** What frightened the enemy was not the cavalry; it was that the law was the same for the
ruler's son.
**Hands-up?** Ask: if you sent a man to look at an army, what would you want him to count? Then read
what this one actually reported.

### ISA/E-C9 · Khālid ؓ hands the command round
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** the morning of اليرموك `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۸۹; الکامل ج۲ ص۲۵۵]` ·
**Map:** the Yarmūk. Roman camp at al-Wāqūṣa with the ravine at its back; the Muslim line sitting
across the only road out.
**What happened:** The Muslim armies had come to the Yarmūk still divided — four commanders, each
over his own men, nobody over all of them. On the morning of the battle Khālid ؓ stood up and made a
proposal. He did not ask for the command. He proposed that they pass it round: one of us today,
another tomorrow, another the day after, until every one of us has held it — and let me have today.
They made him commander, expecting the thing to last a long time. It lasted one day.
**The statement:**
> إن هذا يوم من أيام الله، لا ينبغي فيه الفخر ولا البغي … فتعالوا فلنتعاور الإمارة فليكن عليها بعضُنا
> اليوم والآخر غدًا والآخر بعد غد، حتى يتأمَّر كلكم، ودعوني اليوم أليكم
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۸۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3235
> *English:* "This is a day of God's days — there should be no boasting in it and no aggression …
> So come, let us take the command in turn: one of us today, another tomorrow, another the day
> after, until every one of you has commanded. And let me have charge of you today."
**عبرت:** The man who asked for one day got the day; a man who had demanded the whole command might
have got neither.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C10 · "Armies are made many by victory"
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** اليرموك `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۹۱–۹۲]` · **Map:** the numbers panel goes
up beside the map — with the caveat line under it.
**What happened:** Khālid ؓ had come in from Iraq and was looking at the Roman lines. A Christian Arab
standing near him said out loud what everyone could see: how many the Romans are, and how few the
Muslims. He got an answer that had nothing to do with counting.
**The statement:**
> ويلك، أتخوّفني بالروم؟ وإنما تكثر الجنود بالنصر، وتقل بالخذلان لا بعدد الرجال
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3238
> *English:* "Woe to you — are you frightening me with the Romans? Armies are made many by victory
> and few by abandonment, not by the number of men."
**عبرت:** Counting the enemy is useful; being governed by the count is not.
**Hands-up?** Ask the room to guess the two army sizes. Take three guesses. Then show that the books
themselves give three different answers, and say why.

### ISA/E-C11 · Jirja and the two rakʿas
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** اليرموك `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۹۵–۹۶]` · **Map:** no change; a single
point between the two lines.
**What happened:** One of the senior Roman commanders, Jirja, rode out from his own line and called
Khālid ؓ to him. They met between the armies, close enough that their horses' necks crossed. He asked
one question: is it true God sent your Prophet a sword from heaven, and gave it to you, so that you
never draw it without routing them? Khālid ؓ said: no. Then why are you called the Sword of God? And
Khālid ؓ answered by telling him what he had been — one of the men who called the Prophet ﷺ a liar and
kept away from him. Jirja kept asking. What do you call people to. What if a man refuses. What does a
man get who joins you today, having missed everything. Khālid ؓ said: the same reward — and better,
because you did not see what we saw and you still came. At that Jirja turned his shield over and
crossed to the Muslim side. Khālid ؓ took him to his tent, poured a waterskin over him, and prayed two
rakʿas with him. Jirja was killed later that same day.
**The statement:**
> وأُصيب جرجة ﵀ ولم يصل لله إلا تلك الركعتين مع خالد
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3242
> *English:* "And Jirja was killed, and he never prayed to God but those two rakʿas with Khālid."
**عبرت:** A whole life can turn on one honest question asked of an opponent instead of about him.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C12 · The courier in the middle of the battle
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** اليرموك, and the death of أبو بكر ؓ on Monday, 8 nights remaining of
جمادى الآخرة 13 AH `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۹۵، ص۱۰۲]` · **Map:** a small arrow from Medina to the
Yarmūk — the news travelling while the battle is being fought.
**What happened:** In the middle of the fighting a courier arrived from the Ḥijāz and was brought to
Khālid ؓ. Khālid ؓ asked him what the news was. The man told him quietly: Abū Bakr ؓ is dead. ʿUmar ؓ
is caliph. And Abū ʿUbayda ؓ has been put over the armies in your place. Khālid ؓ heard both pieces of
news at once. He said, loudly enough for the men to hear, "You have done well." Then he took the
letter, put it in his quiver, and went back to running the battle. The army learned neither thing
until it was over. Ibn Kathīr رحمہ اللہ records what happened when they did: they did not rejoice at
the spoil as much as they grieved for Abū Bakr ؓ.
**The statement:**
> فأسرّها خالدٌ ولم يُبْد ذلك للناس لئلا يحصل ضعفٌ ووهنٌ في تلك الحال، وقال له والناسُ يسمعون: أحسنتَ
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۵ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3241
> *English:* "Khālid kept it secret and did not show it to the men, lest weakness and faltering set
> in at such a moment — and he said to him, with the men listening: 'You have done well.'"
**عبرت:** He was told he had been replaced and finished the day's work anyway; that is what obedience
looks like before anyone is watching.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C13 · Damascus, taken two ways at once
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** mid رجب 14 AH per the majority `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۰۸]`; the storming
`[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۰۵; الکامل ج۲ ص۲۶۹–۲۷۰]` · **Map:** Damascus falls. Colour it **half and
half** — that is the whole point of the card.
**What happened:** The siege had gone through the winter and stalled. Then a son was born to the
Byzantine commander of the city, and he made a feast; the men on the wall ate, drank and slept. One
man noticed. Khālid ؓ, Ibn Kathīr رحمہ اللہ says, neither slept nor let anyone else sleep, and had
scouts reporting the state of the garrison morning and evening. He had already made ladders out of
rope. That night his party swam the moat with waterskins at their necks, fixed the ladders to the
battlements, climbed, and shouted the takbīr from the top of the wall. He came down inside, killed the
gatekeepers and cut the bolts. At the same hour, at the other gates, the townspeople — hearing the
noise and not knowing what it was — accepted the terms the Muslims had been offering all along and
opened their own gates. The commanders met in the middle of the city: one side had come in by force,
the other by treaty.
**The statement:**
> فَالْتَقَى خَالِدٌ وَالْقُوَّادُ فِي وَسَطِهَا، هَذَا قَتْلًا وَنَهْبًا، وَهَذَا صَفْحًا وَتَسْكِينًا، فَأَجْرَوْا نَاحِيَةَ خَالِدٍ
> مَجْرَى الصُّلْحِ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۷۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/961
> *English:* "Khālid and the other commanders met in the middle of the city — this one killing and
> plundering, that one pardoning and calming — so they applied the terms of the treaty to Khālid's
> side as well."
**عبرت:** Where they could not tell which had come first, they gave the city the gentler ruling — and
called it caution.
**Hands-up?** Ask: if half a city surrenders and half is stormed on the same night, what are the
terms? Take answers. Then give the Companions' own answer.

### ISA/E-C14 · Fiḥl — the mud they laid themselves
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** 13 or 14 AH; the sequence relative to Damascus is disputed `[SOURCED —
البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۱۲–۱۱۳; الکامل ج۲ ص۲۷۰]` · **Map:** Fiḥl / Baysān in the Ghawr, below the Jordan.
**What happened:** The Romans who had gathered at Fiḥl did something clever. They opened the
irrigation channels and flooded the ground between themselves and the Muslim camp until it was deep
mud, so that no charge could reach them. The Arabs afterwards called that whole campaign "the muddy
one." Then, thinking the Muslims must be off their guard, they attacked in the night. But the
commander there was Shuraḥbīl b. Ḥasana ؓ, who never spent a night or a morning out of battle order.
The camp came up as one man. The fighting ran through the night and the whole of the next day. When
the Romans finally broke, the rout carried them straight back into their own mud.
**The statement:**
> وأسلمتهم هزيمتهم إلى ذلك الوحل الذي كانوا قد كادوا به المسلمين فغرَّقهم الله فيه
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۱۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3259
> *English:* "And their rout delivered them into the very mud with which they had meant to trap the
> Muslims, and God drowned them in it."
**عبرت:** The trap a man builds is still there when he is the one running.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C15 · The removal, and what the two men said
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** 13 AH, on ʿUmar ؓ's accession `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۰۳، ص۲۳۶; سیر
ج۱ ص۳۷۳، ص۳۷۸]` · **Map:** no change. The name over Syria changes; nothing else does.
**What happened:** The first thing ʿUmar ؓ did as caliph was to remove Khālid ؓ from the command of
Syria and put Abū ʿUbayda ؓ in his place. The letter was blunt: take his turban off his head and
divide his property with him. Abū ʿUbayda ؓ carried it out — the books say he divided even the pair of
sandals, taking one and leaving him the other. Khālid ؓ said one sentence while it was being done: I
hear and obey the Commander of the Faithful. And then, at the handover, each of the two men said
something about the other — and neither of them said it in his own words. Each quoted the Prophet ﷺ.
**The statement:**
> فقال خالد: بعث إليكم أمين هذه الأمة، سمعتُ رسول الله ﷺ يقول: "أمينُ هذه الأمة أبو عبيدة بن الجراح".
> فقال أبو عبيدة: سمعت رسول الله ﷺ يقول: "خالدٌ سيفٌ من سيوف الله، نِعْمَ فَتى العشيرة"
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۲۳۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3382
> *English:* Khālid said: "He has sent you the trustee of this community — I heard the Messenger of
> God ﷺ say: 'The trustee of this community is Abū ʿUbayda b. al-Jarrāḥ.'" And Abū ʿUbayda said: "I
> heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: 'Khālid is a sword among the swords of God — what an excellent
> young man of the clan.'"
**عبرت:** Each of them met the other's promotion by quoting the Prophet ﷺ in the other's favour; that
is what settled it, and it is what should settle it for us.
**Hands-up?** no
*(Speaker note: narrate, do not judge. If the room pushes on why, go straight to E-C16 and stop
there.)*

### ISA/E-C16 · "I did not remove him for any suspicion"
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** 15 AH (after Qinnasrīn), and again in writing in 17 AH `[SOURCED —
البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۵۳; الکامل ج۲ ص۳۶۰; سیر ج۱ ص۳۷۸]` · **Map:** no change.
**What happened:** After Khālid ؓ had been removed from the command he stayed in Syria and went on
fighting under Abū ʿUbayda ؓ. At Qinnasrīn he took the town, and when the reports reached Medina,
ʿUmar ؓ said something about Abū Bakr ؓ, and about himself. Two years later, when he removed Khālid ؓ
a second time, he did not leave the matter to talk: he wrote a circular to every garrison city
setting out his reason. Both times the reason is the same, and neither time is it a charge of
wrongdoing.
**The statement:**
> يرحمُ الله أبا بكر، كان أعلمَ بالرجالِ مني، واللّهِ إنِّي لم أعزلْهُ عن ريبةٍ ولكن خشيتُ أن يُوكلَ
> الناسُ إليه
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۵۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3299
> *English:* "May God have mercy on Abū Bakr — he knew men better than I do. By God, I did not
> remove him for any suspicion, but I feared that people would come to rely on him."
**عبرت:** A commander can be removed for being too successful, and both men can be right.
**Hands-up?** no

### ISA/E-C17 · The death on the bed
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** 21 AH, at Homs `[SOURCED — سیر ج۱ ص۳۶۷، ص۳۸۱، ص۳۸۳; البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۳۷
(the «ما في جسدي شبر» version)، ص۲۳۹ (the statement quoted below, and the women weeping)]` ·
**Map:** a last pin at Homs — where the man who crossed the Samāwa is buried.
**What happened:** He died in bed, and he minded. He had looked for death in the places death is
found, he said, and it had not been decreed for him. He was asked what of his own deeds he hoped in
most, and he named neither Yarmūk nor Damascus nor Iraq — he named one cold night spent under his
shield in the rain, waiting for the light. Then he gave his instructions: when I am dead, take my
weapons and my horse and make them equipment in the way of God. He left nothing else — a horse, his
weapons, and a servant. When ʿUmar ؓ heard, on the Mecca road, he said "innā li-Llāh" over and over
and lowered his head. And when the women of Banū al-Mughīra gathered in his house to weep and someone
suggested the caliph forbid them, ʿUmar ؓ refused.
**The statement:**
> وما من عملي شيءٌ أرْجَى عندي بعد لا إله إلا الله من ليلة بتُّها وأنا مُتَترّسٌ والسماء تهلّني ننتظر
> الصبح حتى نُغير على الكفار
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۲۳۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3385
> *English:* "There is nothing of my deeds I hope in more, after 'there is no god but God', than one
> night I passed under my shield with the rain pouring on me, waiting for the dawn."
**عبرت:** The deed he hoped in was the one no one had watched.
**Hands-up?** Ask: of everything this man did, which act do you think he hoped in most? Take three
answers. Then read his own.

### ISA/E-C18 · What the Romans said about why they lost
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** after اليرموك `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۹۹]` · **Map:** Heraclius withdraws
from Homs, then Antioch, then out of Syria altogether — the Roman line pulls back off the board.
**What happened:** The defeated troops came back to Heraclius. He asked them a plain question: are
these men not human beings like you? They said yes. Are you not more numerous? They said many times
over. Then why do you keep getting routed? One of their elders answered, and the answer was a list —
half of it about the Muslims and half of it about themselves. Heraclius did not argue with him.
**The statement:**
> من أجل أنهم يقومون الليل ويصومون النهار، ويوفون بالعهد، ويأمرون بالمعروف، وينهون عن المنكر،
> ويتناصفون بينهم، ومن أجل أنا نشرب الخمر، ونزني، ونركب الحرام، وننقض العهد، ونغْصب ونظلم … فقال: أنت
> صدقتني
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3245
> *English:* "Because they stand at night and fast by day, keep their covenants, command good and
> forbid wrong, and deal justly among themselves — and because we drink wine, commit adultery, ride
> into the forbidden, break our covenants, seize and oppress." … He said: "You have told me the
> truth."
**عبرت:** They were beaten, and they knew exactly why; knowing it is not the same as changing it.
**Hands-up?** no
*(Speaker note: Ibn Kathīr رحمہ اللہ transmits this from a collection outside the six books. Cite it
to al-Bidāya, and say "the book records." The narrower, better-known line on the same page — "by
night monks, by day horsemen" — is the safer one if only one is used.)*

### ISA/E-C19 · The Romans in chains
**Tier:** CUT · **When:** ذات السلاسل 12 AH, and again at اليرموك `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۶۵، ص۸۹]` ·
**Map:** none.
**What happened:** Twice the sources describe an enemy army chaining its own ranks together so that
no man could run. At Kāẓima the Persians did it, and Ibn Kathīr records that the Muslims took it as a
bad omen against them and said so out loud. At the Yarmūk the figure given is tens of thousands
chained in tens — though on the same page Sayf gives a much smaller figure and Ibn Kathīr writes
"God knows best" after it. At the end, when the Roman line broke, the chains did what chains do.
**The statement:**
> فَتيمَّنَ المسلمون بذلك وقالوا هذا طائر مشؤوم قيدوا نفوسهم بالحديد
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۶۵ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3211
> *English:* "The Muslims took it as a good omen for themselves and said: this is an ill-omened
> bird — they have shackled their own souls with iron."
**عبرت:** An army that has to chain its men in place has already told you what it thinks of them.
**Hands-up?** no
*(CUT because the chained-ranks detail is the part of the Yarmūk numbers most likely to be
challenged, and because it adds nothing the evening needs. Recorded so it is not re-researched.)*

### ISA/E-C20 · The judge and the reciter
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the morning of اليرموك `[SOURCED — البدایہ ج۷ ص۸۹–۹۰]` · **Map:** the
battle-order diagram — centre, wings, cavalry behind each wing, Abū ʿUbayda ؓ behind everyone.
**What happened:** When the line was drawn up at the Yarmūk, the appointments made that morning are
worth reading in full. Qubāth b. Ashyam ؓ over the scouts. ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd ؓ over the spoils.
Abū Sufyān ؓ to walk the ranks exhorting. And two more that no army list would think to include: Abū
al-Dardāʾ ؓ as the judge of the day, and al-Miqdād ؓ to ride round the ranks reciting Sūrat al-Anfāl
and the verses of jihād. Abū ʿUbayda ؓ, on Khālid ؓ's advice, moved himself out of the centre and
stood behind the whole army — so that any man who broke would see him and turn back out of shame.
**The statement:**
> والقاضي يومئذ أبو الدرداء، وقاصّهم الذي يعظُهم ويحثُّهم على القتال أبو سفيان بن حرب، وقارؤهم الذي
> يدور على الناس فيقرأ سورة الأنفال وآياتِ الجهاد المِقْداد بن الأسود
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۸۹–۹۰ · the list begins at https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3235 (ص۸۹) and
> runs on to https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3236 (ص۹۰)
> *English:* "And the judge that day was Abū al-Dardāʾ; the one who preached to them and urged them
> to fight was Abū Sufyān b. Ḥarb; and their reciter, who went round the men reading Sūrat al-Anfāl
> and the verses of jihād, was al-Miqdād b. al-Aswad."
**عبرت:** They appointed a judge before the battle, not after it.
**Hands-up?** Ask: what posts would you fill before a battle? Take answers. Then read the list.

---

## القادسية, the embassy to رستم, and المدائن

*Source note: [`qadisiyya-madain-and-the-embassy.md`](../docs/research/qadisiyya-madain-and-the-embassy.md) — read it for the pages behind these cards.*

### QMA/E-Q1 · Nobody would go to Persia
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۳ھ, the morning after أبو بكر ؓ was buried — the first days of عمر ؓ's
caliphate `[SOURCED]`. **The month is not on either page:** البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۱۴ says only *«ودُفن ليلةَ
الثلاثاء»* and الکامل ج۲ ص۲۷۳ *«اللَّيْلَةِ الَّتِي مَاتَ فِيهَا أَبُو بَكْرٍ»*. **جمادى الآخرة** is
`[STANDARD]` *(to verify)* and must not be printed as sourced ·
**Map:** المدینہ lights. A single dotted arrow points north-east toward العراق and stops short.
**What happened:** أبو بكر ؓ was buried at night. The next morning عمر ؓ stood up and asked for
volunteers to go and fight Persia. Nobody moved. He asked again the second day. Nobody moved. He
asked a third time, while men were still filing past to give him their pledge. Still nobody. Ibn
al-Athīr says plainly why: of all the fronts, Persia was the heaviest on the Muslims and the most
hateful to them, because of the severity of that empire and its power and its subjugation of
nations. On the fourth day one man stood up. After that, the rest followed.
**The statement:**
> ثُمَّ نَدَبَ النَّاسَ وَهُوَ يُبَايِعُهُمْ، ثَلَاثًا، وَلَا يَنْتَدِبُ أَحَدٌ إِلَى فَارِسَ، وَكَانُوا أَثْقَلَ الْوُجُوهِ عَلَى الْمُسْلِمِينَ، وَأَكْرَهَهُمْ إِلَيْهِمْ لِشِدَّةِ سُلْطَانِهِمْ وَشَوْكَتِهِمْ وَقَهْرِهِمُ الْأُمَمَ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۷۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/964
> *English:* "Then he called the people while taking their pledge — three times — and no one
> volunteered for Persia. They were the heaviest of all fronts on the Muslims, and the most hateful
> to them, because of the severity of their rule and their power and their subjugation of nations."
**عبرت:** Fear in a believing community is not a scandal; what is asked of it is that somebody
finally stand up.
**Hands-up?** "Who here thinks the Companions were never afraid of anything?"

---

### QMA/E-Q2 · The command goes to the first man who volunteered
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۳ھ, **the fourth day** of the call for volunteers — that is all either
page gives (البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۱۴; الکامل ج۲ ص۲۷۳) `[SOURCED]`. **No month is stated in the sources for
this**; do not print one · **Map:** the arrow from المدینہ to
العراق is now solid; a marker for **الحيرة**.
**What happened:** The man who stood up on the fourth day was أبو عُبَيد بن مسعود الثقفي. He was not
a Companion. عمر ؓ put him in command of the whole army — over men who had fought at Badr. People
objected: appoint one of the Companions. عمر ؓ refused. God raised the Companions by their
precedence, he said; if another man does what they did while they hang back, he has the better right
to command. He would appoint no one but the first to answer. He then called أبو عبيد, سعد بن عُبَيد ؓ
and سَليط بن قيس ؓ — سَليط a man of Badr — and told the last two to their faces: *had you outstripped
him, I would have appointed you.* And to أبو عبيد, about سَليط, he gave a second reason: Salīṭ was too
quick to war, and haste in war ruins an army.
**The statement:**
> وَاللَّهِ لَا أُؤَمِّرُ عَلَيْهِمْ إِلَّا أَوَّلَهُمُ انْتِدَابًا
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۷۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/964
> *English:* "By God, I will appoint over them none but the first of them to volunteer."
**عبرت:** Precedence is earned again every time it is claimed; it does not carry forward on its own.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q3 · The Bridge — the defeat that opened the Iraq front
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** شعبان ۱۳ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** a river line drawn across the arrow; the
marker for **قُسّ الناطف / الجسر**, west of the Euphrates. The advance stops and pulls back.
**What happened:** The Persians came to the river with elephants and the great standard of the
empire. They sent word across: either you cross to us, or we cross to you. His men told him not to
cross. He crossed. On the far bank the ground was tight and the Arab horses would not go near the
elephants. أبو عبيد ordered the elephants cut down and went at the great white one himself with his
sword. It trampled him and stood over his body. Seven men of Thaqīf, whom he had named in advance,
took the banner one after another, and each of them was killed. Then المثنى بن حارثة ؓ took it. Men
crowded back onto the bridge and it broke. About four thousand were killed or drowned; two thousand
scattered into the countryside; three thousand were left.
**The statement:**
> فقال المسلمون لأميرهم أبي عبيد: أُؤْمُرْهُمْ فليعبروا هم إلينا. فقال: ما هُم بأجرأ على الموت منّا، ثم اقتحمَ إليهم
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۱۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3262
> *English:* "The Muslims said to their commander Abū ʿUbayd: order them, and let them cross to us.
> He said: they are no bolder in the face of death than we are — and he plunged across to them."
**عبرت:** Courage that refuses counsel is not courage; it is the same fault wearing better clothes.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q4 · «أنا فئة كل مسلم» — the caliph takes the blame
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** شعبان ۱۳ھ, days after the Bridge `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** small figures
moving back toward المدینہ; المدینہ lights.
**What happened:** Some of the survivors did not stop at the army. They kept going, all the way back
to Medina, and then some of them scattered into the countryside because they were too ashamed to be
seen. عمر ؓ was told. It weighed on him — and what he said was not a reprimand. He absolved every
Muslim, said he himself was the refuge every one of them was entitled to fall back on, and then
asked God's mercy on أبو عبيد, saying that if the man had fallen back to him, he would have been a
refuge for him too. Ibn Kathīr's summary is a single clause: he did not reproach the people.
**The statement:**
> اللَّهُمَّ إِنَّ كُلَّ مُسْلِمٍ فِي حِلٍّ مِنِّي، أَنَا فِئَةُ كُلِّ مُسْلِمٍ، يَرْحَمُ اللَّهُ أَبَا عُبَيْدٍ! لَوْ كَانَ انْحَازَ إِلَيَّ لَكُنْتُ لَهُ فِئَةً
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۷۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/969
> *English:* "O God, every Muslim is absolved by me. I am the refuge of every Muslim. May God have
> mercy on Abū ʿUbayd — had he fallen back to me, I would have been a refuge for him."
**عبرت:** A leader who will not stand under his people's defeat has no right to stand on top of
their victories.
**Hands-up?** "What would most rulers have done with an army that ran?"

---

### QMA/E-Q5 · عمر ؓ is talked out of leading the campaign himself
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۱ المحرم ۱۴ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** عمر ؓ's own marker moves a short way
out of المدینہ to **صِرار**, then returns; a new marker, سعد ؓ, sets out for العراق.
**What happened:** On the first day of Muharram, عمر ؓ rode out of Medina at the head of the army,
intending to fight Persia in person. He camped at a water three miles out, left علي ؓ over Medina,
took عثمان ؓ and the senior Companions with him, and called a council. Everyone agreed with him.
One man did not: عبد الرحمن بن عوف ؓ said that if the caliph were broken, Muslims everywhere on
earth would be weakened — send a man, and go home. عمر ؓ asked whom. Ibn ʿAwf said: I have found
him — the lion in his claws, Saʿd ibn Mālik az-Zuhrī. عمر ؓ and the people accepted it, and he went
back to Medina.
**The statement:**
> إنِّي أخشى إن كسرتَ أن يضعفَ المسلمون في سائر أقطار الأرض، وإني أرى أن تبعث رجلًا وترجع أنت إلى المدينة
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۲۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3275
> *English:* "I fear that if you are broken, the Muslims will be weakened in every quarter of the
> earth. My view is that you send a man, and that you yourself return to Medina."
**عبرت:** The one man in the room who disagrees is worth the whole room, if he is listened to.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q6 · «لا يغرنَّك من الله أن قيل: خالُ رسول الله ﷺ»
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** المحرم ۱۴ھ, at the parting `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none — this is a
statement slide.
**What happened:** سعد بن أبي وقاص ؓ was a Zuhrī, of the clan of the Prophet's ﷺ own mother. People
called him "the maternal uncle of the Messenger of God ﷺ." He was one of the Ten, one of the first
to accept Islam, present at Badr and Ḥudaybiya. عمر ؓ handed him an empire to conquer, and the first
thing he said to him was that none of that would help him. God does not blot out an evil with an
evil, he told him; He blots out an evil with a good. Between God and any man there is no kinship
except obedience. And then the line that flattens every rank in the army at once: the noble and the
low are equal before God, He is their Lord and they are His servants.
**The statement:**
> يا سعد بن وهيب لا يغرنَّك من الله أن قيل: خالُ رسول الله ﷺ وصاحبُه، فإنَّ اللهَ لا يمحو السيِّءَ بالسيِّءَ، ولكن يمحو السيِّءَ بالحسنِ، وإن الله ليس بينه وبين أحدٍ نسبٌ إلا بطاعته، فالناس شريفُهم ووضيعُهم في ذات اللهِ سواء، الله ربهم وهم عبادُه
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۲۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3275
> *English:* "O Saʿd ibn Wuhayb: let it not deceive you before God that it is said, 'the maternal
> uncle of the Messenger of God ﷺ and his companion.' For God does not blot out an evil with an
> evil; He blots out an evil with a good. And between God and any man there is no kinship except by
> obedience to Him. People, the noble of them and the low of them, are equal before God: He is their
> Lord and they are His servants."
**عبرت:** A relationship to a righteous man is a gift to be grateful for, never a claim to be traded
on.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q7 · رِبْعي بن عامر ؓ walks his horse across the carpet
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۴ھ (the year is disputed — see E-Q11) `[SOURCED]` · **Map:**
**القادسية** marker; the Persian camp across the water at **العتيق**; a line between the two.
**What happened:** The Persian commander sent word: send us a man to talk to. سعد ؓ gathered a group
to send. رِبْعي بن عامر ؓ said: if we all go, they will see that we have made much of them — send
one man. So he went alone. They had gilded the audience-hall for him: cushions worked with gold,
silk rugs, jewels laid out, the commander on a throne of gold with his crown on. رِbʿī rode in on a
short horse and did not get off until it was standing on the edge of the carpet. He dismounted, slit
two of the cushions, and tied his horse to them with the rope through the slits. They told him to
put down his weapons. He said: I did not come to you; you sent for me. Leave me as I am, or I go
back. They let him through. He walked in leaning on his spear, and left them not one cushion and not
one carpet that he did not tear. When he reached the commander he sat down on the bare floor. They
asked him why. He said: we do not care to sit on your finery. Then they asked him what had brought
them.
**The statement:**
> الله ابتعثنا لنخرج من شاء من عبادة العباد إلى عبادة الله، ومن ضيق الدنيا إلى سعتها، ومن جَور الأديان إلى عدل الإسلام
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۳۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3280
> *English:* "God sent us out to bring whom He wills out of the worship of servants into the worship
> of God; and out of the narrowness of this world into its breadth; and out of the injustice of the
> religions into the justice of Islam."
**عبرت:** A man who is not impressed by a room cannot be bought by it.
**Hands-up?** "One envoy or seven — which would you have sent?"

---

### QMA/E-Q8 · «ولكن المسلمون كالجسد الواحد» — and رستم's own verdict
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** immediately after E-Q7 `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none — statement slide,
paired with E-Q7.
**What happened:** رستم asked for time to consider. رِبْعي ؓ said: how much — a day, two? The
commander said: until I write to our men of judgement and our chiefs. رِبْعي ؓ told him the
Messenger of God ﷺ had laid down that they do not hold off an enemy at the point of contact more
than three days; choose within that. Then رستم asked him: are you their chief? And he said no — and
gave the reason. After he left, رستم turned to his own nobles and asked whether they had ever heard
speech more dignified. They told him not to be taken in by a dog in rags. His answer was the last
word in the room.
**The statement:**
> فقال: أسيدهم أنت؟ قال: لا، ولكن المسلمون كالجسد الواحد يجير أدناهم على أعلاهم … فقال: ويلكم لا تنظروا إلى الثياب، وانظروا إلى الرأي والكلام والسيرة. إن العربَ يستخفّون بالثياب والمأكل، ويصونون الأحساب
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۳۴–۱۳۵ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3280 **و** https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3281
> (the second half straddles the break: «وانظروا إلى الرأي … ويصونون الأحساب» is on ص۱۳۵)
> *English:* "He said: are you their chief? He said: no — but the Muslims are like one body: the
> lowest of them gives protection binding on the highest of them … [Rustam said:] woe to you, do not
> look at the clothes. Look at the judgement and the speech and the conduct. The Arabs make light of
> clothing and food, and they guard their honour."
**عبرت:** A community is judged by what its least important member may promise in its name.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q9 · «إِنَّا مَعْشَرَ الْعَرَبِ لَا نَسْتَعْبِدُ بَعْضَنَا بَعْضًا»
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the third day of the embassies `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none — statement
slide.
**What happened:** On the third day it was المغيرة بن شعبة ؓ. He walked up and sat down on the
throne, beside the Persian commander. They sprang on him, dragged him off and shoved him about. He
did not apologise. He told them that reports of their good sense had reached the Arabs, and that he
had never seen a more foolish people; that Arabs do not enslave one another, and he had assumed they
shared with their own people as Arabs did. Then he told them what he had learnt that day. Ibn
al-Athīr records who said what next: the low-born of the court said the Arab had spoken the truth;
the landholders said he had thrown out a word their slaves would never stop reaching for.
**The statement:**
> إِنَّا مَعْشَرَ الْعَرَبِ لَا نَسْتَعْبِدُ بَعْضَنَا بَعْضًا … الْيَوْمَ عَلِمْتُ أَنَّكُمْ مُغَلَّبُونَ، وَأَنَّ مُلْكًا لَا يَقُومُ عَلَى هَذِهِ السِّيرَةِ وَلَا عَلَى هَذِهِ الْعُقُولِ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۲۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/990
> *English:* "We Arabs do not enslave one another … Today I have learnt that you will be overcome,
> and that a kingdom does not stand on this conduct or on these minds."
**عبرت:** An order that has to keep men beneath it in order to stand is already standing on
something that is giving way.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q10 · «فبعث الله إلينا رجلًا معروفًا نعرف نسبَه» — before the king
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** before the battle `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the delegation's line runs from
**القادسية** all the way to **المدائن** and back.
**What happened:** A second delegation went past the army to the king himself. النعمان بن مقرن ؓ put
the three choices. The king answered with contempt: he knew of no people on earth more wretched,
fewer in number or worse in their dealings with one another than the Arabs; Persia had kept border
villages simply to keep them off. The delegation fell silent. Then المغيرة بن شعبة ؓ stood up and
agreed with every word of it. Nobody was in a worse state than we were, he said. We ate beetles and
scorpions and snakes and counted it food. We lived on bare ground and wore what we spun from camel
hair. Our religion was that we killed one another and wronged one another, and a man would bury his
daughter alive rather than have her eat his food. And then he turned it.
**The statement:**
> فبعث الله إلينا رجلًا معروفًا نعرف نسبَه ونعرفُ وجهَه ومولدَه، فأرضه خير أرضنا، وحَسَبُه خيرُ أحسابنا، وبيتُه خيرُ بيوتنا، وقبيلتُه خيرُ قبائنا، وهو نفسه كان خيرَنا في الحال التي كان فيها أصدقنا وأحلمنا
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۳۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3283
> *English:* "Then God sent to us a man we knew — we knew his lineage, and we knew his face, and we
> knew his birth. His land was the best of our land, his descent the best of our descents, his house
> the best of our houses, his tribe the best of our tribes; and he himself was the best of us in the
> state we were in, the most truthful of us and the most forbearing."
**عبرت:** A man who is honest about where he came from can afford to say plainly what changed him.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q11 · The commander who could not sit up
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** the four days of القادسية, ۱۴ھ — *and the tradition disputes the year:
14, 15 or 16* `[SOURCED for the dispute]` · **Map:** **القادسية**; the Muslim line drawn hard up
against a small fort behind it.
**What happened:** When the lines faced each other, سعد ؓ could not ride and could not sit. He had
sciatica and boils. He lay face-down on a cushion on the roof of a small fort, looking out over the
army and giving orders, and he handed the actual fighting to خالد بن عُرفطة. The Muslim line was at
the foot of his wall — Ibn al-Athīr says that if the line had left him uncovered for as long as it
takes to milk a camel, he would have been taken alive; and he never shut the fort's door. A soldier
made a couplet mocking him for it. Saʿd came down to the men, showed them the open sores on his
thighs and his hips, and they accepted it.
**The statement:**
> فيقالُ: إن سعدًا نزل إلى الناس فاعتذر إليهم مما فيه من القروح في فخذيه وأليتيه، فعذره الناسُ
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۴۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3287
> *English:* "It is said that Saʿd came down to the people and excused himself to them for the sores
> in his thighs and hips — and the people excused him."
**عبرت:** Explaining yourself to the people you lead costs less than the rumour you leave standing.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q12 · Four days, and a night called الهرير
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** أرماث · أغواث · عِماس · ليلة الهرير `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the القادسية
inset; four day-markers along one line, the third and fourth joined by a night.
**What happened:** Saʿd led the noon prayer, then addressed the army and recited the verse about the
earth being inherited by God's righteous servants. The reciters read Sūrat al-Anfāl. He had told
them: at the first takbīr, make ready; at the second, put on your gear; at the third, let the
horsemen stir the men; at the fourth, advance together and say lā ḥawla wa-lā quwwata illā billāh.
They fought to nightfall on the first day; the second day and most of its night; the third day; and
then, through the third night, they stopped speaking altogether. Ibn al-Athīr says that is where the
night's name comes from — they only snarled. The sound of iron in it, he says, was like a smithy,
all night until morning.
**The statement:**
> قِيلَ: إِنَّمَا سُمِّيَتْ بِذَلِكَ لِتَرْكِهِمُ الْكَلَامَ إِنَّمَا كَانُوا يَهِرُّونَ هَرِيرًا
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۳۱۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1002
> *English:* "It is said it was named that because they left off speech; they only snarled."
**عبرت:** Endurance is not one heroic hour; it is the fourth day, when nobody is watching and
everybody is finished.
**Hands-up?** "How many nights without sleep before you would have gone home?"

---

### QMA/E-Q13 · The elephants, and the two spears
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the third day, يوم عِماس `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the القادسية inset; a
symbol for the elephant line in the Persian centre, then removed.
**What happened:** Thirty-three elephants, and among them the old white elephant of Sābūr, which the
rest of the herd followed. Eighteen stood in the Persian centre. The Arab horses would not face
them. On the third day سعد ؓ sent two orders: القعقاع and عاصم, sons of ʿAmr, take charge of the
white one for me; Ḥammāl and ar-Ribbīl, take charge of the mangy one. The first pair put their two
spears into the white elephant's eye; it threw its driver and dropped its trunk, and القعقاع cut it
down. The second pair blinded the other, and it turned, threw itself into the river, and the whole
herd followed it — straight out through the Persian line and away. Once the elephants were gone,
Ibn Kathīr says simply that the Companions destroyed them and those on them and put out their eyes.
**The statement:**
> وقد قاسوا من الفيلة بالنسبة إلى الخيول العربية بسبب نفرتها منها أمرًا بليغًا، وقد أباد الصحابة الفيلةَ ومنْ عليها، وقلعوا عيونها
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۳۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3285
> *English:* "They suffered a great deal from the elephants, given the Arab horses' terror of them;
> and the Companions destroyed the elephants and those on them, and put out their eyes."
**عبرت:** What terrifies an army is usually one thing, and it usually has a weak point somebody has
to be sent to find.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q14 · A wind, a mule, and the end of رستم
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** past noon on the fourth day `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the Persian centre
breaks; arrows scatter north toward **المدائن** and east toward **نهاوند**.
**What happened:** Past noon on the fourth day the Persian centre opened. A violent wind got up,
tore the canopy off Rustam's throne and carried it into the water. He got down from the throne and
went to shelter in the shade of a baggage mule. A Muslim named Hilāl cut the ropes of the load
above him — not knowing he was there — and a pannier came down on him and broke his back. He made
for the river and threw himself in; Hilāl went in after him, dragged him out by the legs and killed
him, then climbed onto the empty throne and shouted. The Persian centre broke. Thirty thousand
chained infantry could not run, and did not survive.
**The statement:**
> ثُمَّ صَعِدَ السَّرِيرَ وَقَالَ: قَتَلْتُ رُسْتُمَ وَرَبِّ الْكَعْبَةِ! إِلَيَّ إِلَيَّ! فَأَطَافُوا بِهِ وَكَبَّرُوا
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۳۱۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1004
> *English:* "Then he climbed the throne and said: I have killed Rustam, by the Lord of the Kaʿba! To
> me! To me! — and they gathered round him and made takbīr."
**عبرت:** Empires do not usually fall to a plan; they fall on the day their weight finally shifts.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q15 · أبو مِحْجَن ؓ comes back to his chains
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** during the four days `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none — inside the fort.
**What happened:** There was a man in irons inside Saʿd's own fort, under punishment. From where he
sat he could hear the fighting. He asked the woman of the household to strike off his fetters and
lend him the commander's horse, and he swore to her that at the end of the day he would come back
and put his own leg back in the shackle. She did it. He fought all day. Saʿd watched from the roof
and half-recognised his own horse and could not make sense of it, because the man was supposed to be
in chains. At dusk the man came back, put his foot in the iron, and sat down. Saʿd came down, found
his horse wet with sweat, asked what had happened, and was told. He forgave him and set him free.
**The statement:**
> فَأَطْلَقَهُ فَقَالَ: اذْهَبْ فَمَا أَنَا مُؤَاخِذُكَ بِشَيْءٍ تَقُولُهُ حَتَّى تَفْعَلَهُ. قَالَ: لَا جَرَمَ، [وَاللَّهِ] لَا أُجِيبُ لِسَانِي إِلَى [صِفَةِ] قَبِيحٍ أَبَدًا!
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۳۰۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1000
> *English:* "He released him and said: go — I will not hold you to account for anything you say
> until you do it. He said: then indeed, by God, I will never again let my tongue answer to
> describing anything shameful."
**عبرت:** A man under punishment is still a man who can keep his word — and a community that
notices that gets him back.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q16 · The caliph on foot under the messenger's camel
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** after القادسية `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the road from العراق to المدینہ;
المدینہ lights.
**What happened:** عمر ؓ could not wait for the news. He questioned every rider he met, and he took
to walking out of Medina up the Iraq road to breathe in whatever word was coming. One day he saw a
rider in the distance and went to meet him. The man told him: God has given the Muslims victory at
al-Qādisiyya — and went on talking. He had no idea who he was talking to. The caliph walked the rest
of the way on foot, beneath the man's mount, listening. Near the town people began greeting ʿUmar by
his title. The rider understood, and was mortified.
**The statement:**
> فقال: يرحمكَ الله يا أمير المؤمنين هلا أعلمتنى أنَّكَ الخليفةُ؟ فقال لا حرج عليك يا أخي
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۴۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3286
> *English:* "He said: God have mercy on you, Commander of the Faithful — why did you not tell me you
> were the caliph? He said: no blame on you, my brother."
**عبرت:** Authority that has to be announced to be felt is not yet authority.
**Hands-up?** "If you had just conquered Persia, how would you expect the news to be received at
home?"

---

### QMA/E-Q17 · «أتخافون من هذه النطفة؟» — crossing the Tigris
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** صفر ۱۶ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the Tigris; **بَهُرَسِير** on the west
bank, **المدائن** on the east; the crossing drawn straight across, not at a bridge.
**What happened:** The Persians had taken every boat across with them. The Tigris was in flood,
black and foaming, and word came that the king was three days from carrying the treasury away. Saʿd
preached on the bank and said he had resolved to cross the water, and the army answered as one: may
God resolve us and you on right guidance — do it. Sixty men went first, then six hundred. One of
them looked at the flood and asked the rest a question, then recited a verse and put his horse in.
The Persians on the far bank watched men floating on the surface of the river and shouted that they
were madmen. Not one man was lost. One cup went into the water and washed up on the bank they were
making for.
**The statement:**
> فقال: أتخافون من هذه النطفة؟ ثم تلا قوله تعالى: ﴿وَمَا كَانَ لِنَفْسٍ أَنْ تَمُوتَ إِلَّا بِإِذْنِ اللَّهِ كِتَابًا مُؤَجَّلًا﴾
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۷۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3316
> *English:* "He said: are you afraid of this drop of water? Then he recited: 'It is not for any soul
> to die except by God's leave, at an appointed term.'"
**عبرت:** Fear measures the obstacle; faith measures the One who appointed the term.
**Hands-up?** "Would you have gone in?"

---

### QMA/E-Q18 · Eight rakʿas in the Īwān of Chosroes
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** صفر ۱۶ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** **المدائن** taken; the first Jumuʿa marker
in Iraq.
**What happened:** They came out of the water and rode into the capital, and there was nobody in it.
The king had taken his family and what he could carry and gone. The White Palace held out three days
and then came down; سلمان الفارسي ؓ made the summons, as he had at Bahurasīr. Saʿd took the great
audience-hall of the Persian kings as a place of prayer. Ibn al-Athīr adds that he did not even
alter the images in it. When he entered he recited four verses of Sūrat ad-Dukhān, walked to the
front of the hall, and prayed the prayer of the conquest — eight rakʿas. The first Friday prayer
ever held in Iraq was prayed there, in Ṣafar of the year sixteen.
**The statement:**
> وحين دخله تلا قوله تعالى: ﴿كَمْ تَرَكُوا مِنْ جَنَّاتٍ وَعُيُونٍ وَزُرُوعٍ وَمَقَامٍ كَرِيمٍ وَنَعْمَةٍ كَانُوا فِيهَا فَاكِهِينَ كَذَلِكَ وَأَوْرَثْنَاهَا قَوْمًا آخَرِينَ﴾
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۷۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3317
> *English:* "When he entered it he recited: 'How many gardens and springs did they leave behind, and
> cornfields and a noble station, and a comfort in which they took delight! So it was; and We
> bequeathed it to another people.'"
**عبرت:** The verse was revealed about Pharaoh's people; a man standing in an emperor's hall read it
about the emperor — and about himself.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q19 · The carpet, and «إِنَّكَ عَفَفْتَ فَعَفَّتِ الرَّعِيَّةُ»
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۶ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the fifth travels from **المدائن** to
**المدینہ**.
**What happened:** Among the spoils was a thing the Arabs called the qiṭf: one carpet, sixty cubits
by sixty, that the Persian kings set out in winter when the flowers were gone so that they could sit
as if in a garden. Its leaves were silk on rods of gold; its blossom was gold and silver; its fruit
was jewels. It could not be divided into fifths, so Saʿd asked the army to give up their share and
let it go whole to Medina, and they agreed. When the regalia of Chosroes was laid in front of ʿUmar,
he said that a people who handed this over were trustworthy indeed — and ʿAlī ؓ told him why they
had. Then ʿUmar asked for advice about the carpet, and ʿAlī ؓ told him that a man owns nothing of
this world but what he has given away, or worn out, or eaten. So ʿUmar cut it up and divided it. A
piece fell to ʿAlī ؓ, and he sold it for twenty thousand.
**The statement:**
> فقال له علي بن أبي طالب: إنك عَفَفْتَ فَعَفَّتْ رَعيتُكَ، ولو رَتَعْتَ لرَتَعتْ
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۷۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3319
> *English:* "ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib said to him: you restrained yourself, so your subjects restrained
> themselves; and had you grazed at large, they would have grazed at large."
**عبرت:** The honesty of a whole administration is usually a copy of one man's habits.
**Hands-up?** "What would you have done with a carpet like that?"

---

### QMA/E-Q20 · The man who would not give his name
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۱۶ھ, at المدائن `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** none — the spoils tent.
**What happened:** The treasure of the Persian kings was being carried in and written down, item by
item, by an officer appointed for it. A man walked in with a casket and put it down. The officer and
the men with him said they had never seen anything like it; nothing already in the store came near
it. They asked him whether he had taken anything from it. He said that were it not for God he would
not have brought it to them at all. They asked him who he was. He refused to say, so that they would
not praise him. They sent a man after him to find out, and he turned out to be ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays.
Saʿd, looking at the whole of it, said that if it were not for the standing that belongs to the men
of Badr he would have said this army was above them.
**The statement:**
> فَقَالَ: وَاللَّهِ لَا أُخْبِرُكُمْ فَتَحْمَدُونِي وَلَكِنِّي أَحْمَدُ اللَّهَ وَأَرْضَى بِثَوَابِهِ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۳۴۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1034
> *English:* "He said: by God, I will not tell you, so that you praise me. I praise God, and I am
> content with His reward."
**عبرت:** The deeds that hold a community together are mostly the ones nobody was meant to find out
about.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q21 · جَلولاء — the charge of one man
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** صفر ۱۶ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** **جلولاء** north-east of المدائن;
**حلوان** beyond it, where the king had gone.
**What happened:** The king fell back to Ḥulwān and left an army dug in behind a ditch at Jalūlāʾ,
ringed with iron caltrops. They swore by fire that they would not run until the Arabs were finished.
ʿUmar ordered Saʿd to stay at al-Madāʾin and send his nephew Hāshim ibn ʿUtba with about twelve
thousand, al-Qaʿqāʿ ibn ʿAmr on the vanguard. On the last day they fought from morning until the
arrows were gone on both sides and the spears were shattered, prayed the noon prayer by gesture
because they could not stop, and then a fresh Persian division came up against a spent Muslim line.
Al-Qaʿqāʿ asked his men whether what they had seen had broken them. They said: we are worn out and
they are fresh. Then, in the dark, he took a body of horsemen straight through to the gate of the
enemy's own ditch and held it, and had a crier tell the army where he was.
**The statement:**
> فقال: بل إنا حاملون عليهم ومُجدُّون في طلبهم، حتى يحكم الله بيننا، فاحملوا عليهم حملةً رجل واحدٍ حتى نخالطهم
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۷۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3322
> *English:* "He said: rather, we are charging them and pressing hard after them, until God judges
> between us. Charge them with the charge of one man, until we are in among them."
**عبرت:** The difference between an exhausted army and a beaten one is usually one man who has not
yet agreed to be beaten.
**Hands-up?** no

---

### QMA/E-Q22 · Why الكوفة and البصرة exist
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** المحرم ۱۷ھ (الكوفة); ۱۴ or ۱۶ھ (البصرة — disputed) `[SOURCED]` ·
**Map:** two new city markers, **الكوفة** west of the Euphrates and **البصرة** to the south — the
first two Muslim cities ever founded.
**What happened:** After the conquest the army stayed at al-Madāʾin, and it began to sicken. A
delegation came to ʿUmar and he asked them why their colour had changed. They said the country did
not agree with them. Saʿd wrote the same: nothing suits the Arabs but land that suits their camels.
So ʿUmar sent back an order with a condition in it that explains both cities at once — find a place
with dry ground and water, and with no sea and no bridge between me and you. Salmān and Ḥudhayfa
went out and found a stretch of red sand and gravel with three monasteries on it, prayed there, and
sent word. The mosque was laid out first. An archer stood in the middle of it and shot an arrow to
each of the four sides, and nothing was built inside where the arrows fell.
**The statement:**
> أَنِ ابْعَثْ سَلْمَانَ وَحُذَيْفَةَ رَائِدَيْنِ فَلْيَرْتَادَا مَنْزِلًا بَرِّيًّا بَحْرِيًّا لَيْسَ بَيْنِي وَبَيْنَكُمْ فِيهِ بَحْرٌ وَلَا جِسْرٌ
> — الکامل فی التاریخ ج۲ ص۳۵۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1043
> *English:* "Send Salmān and Ḥudhayfa as scouts, to seek out a place with dry land and water, with
> no sea and no bridge between me and you."
**عبرت:** A conquest that does not think about how the conquerors will live has not finished
thinking.
**Hands-up?** "Where would you have put the new city?"

---

### QMA/E-Q23 · «فَلَيْسَ بِقَصْرِكَ وَلَكِنَّهُ قَصْرُ الْخَبَالِ»
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۷ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the الكوفة inset; the governor's house beside
the mosque and the treasury.
**What happened:** Saʿd built himself a house next to the mosque, with the treasury behind it. The
markets were loud, and he was heard to say "quiet that little noise" — and people had started calling
the building Saʿd's palace. Word reached Medina. ʿUmar sent Muhammad ibn Maslama with a single
instruction: when you get to Kufa, strike your flint, gather firewood, burn the gate of that palace,
and come straight back. He did it. He would not go in when Saʿd sent for him, so Saʿd came out to
