**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ذو الحجة ۲۳ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** n/a
**What happened:** عمر ؓ was stabbed at the dawn prayer. As he lay dying, people began to praise
him — *you were, and you did, and you were.* He stopped them. Ten years of conquest, the calendar,
the diwan, the provinces — and the assessment he gave of all of it was a merchant's word: break even.
Nothing owed, nothing earned. The only thing he asked to keep was that his companionship of the
Prophet ﷺ should come through undamaged.
**The statement:**
> أما والله وددت أني خرجت منها كفافا لا علي ولا لي وأن صحبة رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم سلمت لي
> — سیر (سير الخلفاء الراشدين) ص۹۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1235
> *English:* "By God, I would love to come out of it break-even — nothing against me and nothing for me — and that my companionship of the Messenger of God ﷺ came through safe."
**عبرت:** The man with the largest ledger in the world hoped only that it would balance.
**Hands-up?** *He conquered an empire. What score would you expect him to give himself?*

---

### GSA/E-ST28 · He rules out his own son, by name
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ذو الحجة ۲۳ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** n/a
**What happened:** Dying, عمر ؓ was pressed to name a successor. His own son عبد الله ؓ was
suggested to him — by his son himself in one account, and by another man in another. He had himself
sat up, and refused, twice, in the sharpest language in the whole record.
**The statement:**
> فما أنا قائل لله إذا سألني عمن أمرت عليهم … لأرددنها إلى الذي دفعها إلي أول مرة، ولوددت أن عليها من هو
> خير مني
> — سیر (سير الخلفاء الراشدين) ص۹۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1238
> *English:* "What am I to say to God when He asks me about the man I set over them? … I shall hand it back to the One who gave it to me in the first place. And I would love it if someone better than me were over it."
**عبرت:** Refusing to hand power to your own family is a decision, and it has to be made out loud, on the record, or it does not hold.
**Hands-up?** *He had a son who was a Companion, and universally respected. Does he appoint him?*
**⚠** Session 4 is where خلافت → ملوکيت is handled. **Do not draw the comparison here.** State this fact, let it stand, and move on.

---

### GSA/E-ST29 · عمر ؓ asks permission to be buried
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ذو الحجة ۲۳ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the حجرة of عائشة ؓ, Medina
**What happened:** With his debts counted — eighty-six thousand, or thereabouts, to be paid from his
own family's property first — عمر ؓ sent his son to عائشة ؓ. Not with an instruction. With a
request. She had been keeping that place for herself.
**The statement:**
> اذهب إلى أم المؤمنين عائشة فقل: يستأذن عمر أن يدفن مع صاحبيه … فقالت: كنت أريده -تعني المكان- لنفسي
> ولأؤثرنه اليوم على نفسي
> — سیر (سير الخلفاء الراشدين) ص۹۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1236
> *English:* "Go to the Mother of the Believers, ʿĀʾisha, and say: ʿUmar asks permission to be buried with his two companions." … She said: "I wanted it for myself — but today I shall certainly prefer him to myself."
**عبرت:** He held the largest office on earth and still had to ask; and she, who could have said no, gave up her own place.
**Hands-up?** no. This is the closing slide. Say nothing after it.

---

### GSA/E-ST30 · أبو عبيدة ؓ will not leave his army in the plague
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** طاعون عمواس — the statement `[SOURCED — الحاكم: «رواته كلهم ثقات»]`, the year `۱۸ھ` `[CONVENTIONAL-ESTIMATE — البدایہ prints its عمواس material under ۱۷ھ; see §3.1]` · **Map:** عَمَواس in فلسطين; then the move up to الجابية
**What happened:** Plague broke out in Syria and عمر ؓ wrote to his commander-in-chief. The letter
did not mention the plague. It said a matter had come up that he had to discuss face to face, and
that أبو عبيدة ؓ should not put the letter down before setting out. أبو عبيدة ؓ read it and knew
exactly what it was: *he wants to keep alive a man who is not going to remain.* He wrote back asking
to be released from the order. When عمر ؓ read the reply he wept, and the people around him asked
if أبو عبيدة ؓ had died. Then عمر ؓ wrote a second letter — move the men to higher ground — and
أبو عبيدة ؓ obeyed that one at once, and was struck as he put his foot in the stirrup.
**The statement:**
> فَحَلِّلْنِي مِنْ عَزِيْمَتك، فَإِنِّي فِي جُنْد ‖ مِنْ أَجْنَادِ المُسْلِمِيْنَ، لاَ أَرْغَبُ بِنَفْسِي
> عَنْهُم. فَلَمَّا قَرَأَ عُمَرُ الكِتَابَ، بَكَى، فَقِيْلَ لَهُ: مَاتَ أَبُو عُبَيْدَةَ؟ قَالَ: لاَ،
> وَكَأَنْ قَد
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء ج۱ ص۱۸–۱۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1444 و 1445
> *English:* "Release me from your insistence. I am in one of the armies of the Muslims, and I will not hold myself above them." … When ʿUmar read the letter he wept. It was said to him: "Has Abū ʿUbayda died?" He said: "No — but as good as."
**عبرت:** He refused the order that would have saved him and obeyed the one that saved his men, and both refusals came from the same principle.
**Hands-up?** *Your commander orders you out of an epidemic. Your men stay. Do you go?*

---

### GSA/E-ST31 · «I wish I were in his skin»
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۳–۱۸ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** n/a
**What happened:** أبو عبيدة بن الجراح ؓ — Amīn hādhihi l-umma, commander-in-chief in Syria,
and a man of Quraysh — stood up in front of his own army and said what his Qurashī descent was worth
against taqwā.
**The statement:**
> يَا أَيُّهَا النَّاسُ! إِنِّي امْرُؤٌ مِنْ قُرَيْشٍ، وَمَا مِنْكُم مِنْ أَحْمَرَ وَلاَ أَسْوَدَ
> يَفْضُلُنِي بِتَقْوَى، إِلاَّ وَدِدْتُ أَنِّي فِي مِسْلاَخِه
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء ج۱ ص۱۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1444
> *English:* "People — I am a man of Quraysh, and there is not one of you, red or black, who surpasses me in fear of God but I wish I were in his skin."
**عبرت:** He did not deny his lineage; he priced it.
**Hands-up?** no. Pair it on the screen with E-ST24 and let the two stand together.

---

### GSA/E-ST32 · خالد ؓ divides his sandals with the treasury
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۳ھ, after اليرموك `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** the Syrian command passing from خالد ؓ to أبو عبيدة ؓ
**What happened:** عمر ؓ became caliph and the first order he gave was to remove Khālid ibn
al-Walīd ؓ from command — at the height of his success, immediately after Yarmūk. أبو عبيدة ؓ
carried it out. The turban came off his head and his property was divided in half with the treasury,
down to one of his two sandals. خالد ؓ said one thing throughout. **Why he was dismissed is
disputed inside our own sources and must not be settled from the platform — see §2.5(d).**
**The statement:**
> فقاسمه أبو عبيدة حتى أخذ [إحدى] (٢) نعليه وترك له الأخرى (٣)، وخالد يقول سمعًا وطاعة لأمير المؤمنين
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۰۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3249
> *English:* Abū ʿUbayda divided with him until he took one of his two sandals and left him the other — and Khālid kept saying: "To hear and to obey, for the Commander of the Believers."
**عبرت:** A state survives its best men only if its best men can be removed, and are willing to be.
**Hands-up?** *The greatest general of the age has just won the war. The new caliph's first order is — what?*

---

### GSA/E-ST33 · خالد ؓ dies in his bed
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۲۱ھ, حمص `[SOURCED — Ibn Ḥajar: «إسناده حسن»]` · **Map:** حمص
**What happened:** خالد بن الوليد ؓ died on a mattress. He said he had looked for death in the
places where it is most likely to be found and had not been given it. Then he named the one deed he
hoped in most, after tawḥīd — and it was not a battle. It was a night spent behind a shield in the
rain, waiting for morning. His last instruction was about his weapon and his horse. When he died he
left nothing but those and a servant.
**The statement:**
> لَقَدْ طَلَبْتُ القَتْلَ مَظَانَّهُ، فَلَمْ يُقَدَّرْ لِي إِلاَّ أَنْ أَمُوْتَ عَلَى فِرَاشِي، وَمَا
> مِنْ عَمَلِي شَيْءٌ أَرْجَى عِنْدِي بَعْدَ التَّوْحِيْدِ مِنْ لَيْلَةٍ بِتُّهَا وَأَنَا مُتَتَرِّسٌ،
> وَالسَّمَاءُ تُهِلُّنِي، نَنْتَظِرُ الصُّبْحَ حَتَّى نُغِيْرَ عَلَى الكُفَّار
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء ج۱ ص۳۸۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1807
> *English:* "I sought death in the places where it is most likely to be found, and it was not decreed for me except to die in my bed. And nothing of my deeds do I hope in more, after tawḥīd, than one night I spent behind my shield with the sky raining on me, waiting for the dawn so that we could ride down on the unbelievers."
**عبرت:** He counted the waiting, not the victories.
**Hands-up?** *Of everything he did, which one night do you think he hoped in most?*
**⚠** Use this wording. The famous «فلا نامت أعين الجبناء» wording runs through الواقدي, and it does **not** say "a hundred battles" — see §X.4.

---

### GSA/E-ST34 · By night monks, by day horsemen
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** ۱۳ھ, before دمشق `[STANDARD — the chain has an unnamed link; see §4.2]` · **Map:** the Muslim camp in the Jordan country, seen from Damascus
**What happened:** The commander of Damascus sent for two Christian Arabs in the market and asked
one of them to go out and look at these people who had come. The man came back and described what he
had seen: lean men on good horses, mending their arrows and straightening their spears; and a noise
over the camp so loud you could not hear the man sitting next to you. The commander turned to his
own people and told them what it meant.
**The statement:**
> أما الليل فرهبان، وأما النهار ففرسان … لو حدَّثت جليسك حديثًا ما فهمه عنك لما علا من أصواتهم بالقرآن
> والذكر … أتاكم منهم ما لا طاقة لكم به
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3245
> *English:* "By night they are monks and by day they are horsemen … and if you spoke to the man sitting beside you he would not catch what you said, for the noise of their voices with the Qurʾān and the remembrance of God." … "There has come upon you from them what you have no strength against."
**عبرت:** What frightened the other side was not the cavalry; it was the noise coming off the camp at night.
**Hands-up?** *If you sent a spy into their camp, what would you expect him to report?*

---

### GSA/E-ST35 · Heraclius asks his own men why they keep losing
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** after اليرموك, ۱۳ھ `[STANDARD — maqṭūʿ, no grading on the page; البدایہ prints it under «سنة ثلاث عشرة», the same year-chapter as E-ST32 and E-ST34]` · **Map:** أنطاكية, with the beaten army coming in
**What happened:** Heraclius was at Antioch when his defeated troops came back. He asked them three
questions: are these people not human like you; are you not more numerous; then why do you run? One
of their elders answered him, and the answer had two halves — what the Muslims do, and what we do.
Heraclius accepted it.
**The statement:**
> من أجل أنهم يقومون الليل ويصومون النهار، ويوفون بالعهد، ويأمرون بالمعروف، وينهون عن المنكر، ويتناصفون
> بينهم، ومن أجل أنا نشرب الخمر، ونزني … وننقض العهد، ونغْصب ونظلم … فقال: أنت صدقتني
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3245
> *English:* "Because they stand at night and fast by day; they keep their covenant; they command what is right and forbid what is wrong; and they deal justly among themselves — and because we drink wine, and commit adultery … and break covenants, and seize and oppress …" He said: "You have told me the truth."
**عبرت:** The explanation the losing side gave for its own defeat was a moral one, and nobody made them say it.
**Hands-up?** *Why do you think they won?* — collect answers, then read this.

---

### GSA/E-ST36 · ربعي بن عامر ؓ walks his horse onto the carpet
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۱۴–۱۵ھ, before القادسية `[STANDARD — VIA سيف/الطبری; see §4.1]` · **Map:** رستم's camp on the Euphrates, facing القادسية
**What happened:** سعد ؓ sent an envoy to Rustam. The hall was laid with gilt cushions and silk;
Rustam was on a throne of gold. ربعي ؓ rode a short horse to the edge of the carpet, tied it to one
of the cushions, and came forward in armour leaning on his spear, so that the spear tore the rugs.
They told him to put down his weapon; he said he had not come to them, they had called him. Then
they asked what had brought him. Later, asked whether he was their chief, he gave the better answer
of the two.
**The statement:**
> الله ابتعثنا لنخرج من شاء من عبادة العباد إلى عبادة الله، ومن ضيق الدنيا إلى سعتها، ومن جَور الأديان إلى
> عدل الإسلام … لا: ولكن المسلمون كالجسد الواحد يجير أدناهم على أعلاهم
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۱۳۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3280
> *English:* "God sent us to bring out whoever wishes, from the worship of servants to the worship of God; from the narrowness of this world to its breadth; and from the injustice of the religions to the justice of Islam." … "No — but the Muslims are like one body: the lowest of them can give protection that binds the highest of them."
**عبرت:** He was not the chief and it made no difference; that was the whole of his answer.
**Hands-up?** no.
**⚠** The most famous sentence of the period, and **not soundly chained**. Introduce with *"as the books tell it"*, never *"he said"*. See §4.1.

---

### GSA/E-ST37 · «Are you making rhymes at me, or reporting?»
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۲۲–۲۳ھ `[STANDARD — VIA سيف/الطبری]` · **Map:** مُكران, the Sind frontier — and the line عمر ؓ drew across it
**What happened:** An officer came back from the far eastern frontier with the fifth of the spoils.
عمر ؓ asked him what the country was like. The man answered in polished rhyming prose — mountain
for plain, a trickle for water, poor dates, a fierce enemy. عمر ؓ cut across him with one
question. The man said he was reporting. So عمر ؓ wrote to the commander not to campaign beyond
Mukrān, and to stop at the river.
**The statement:**
> أرضٌ سهلُها جبلٌ، وماؤُها وَشَلُ (٤)، وتَمْرُها (٥) دَقَلُ (٦)، وعدوُّها بطل … فقال عمر: أسجَّاعٌ أنتَ
> أم مُخْبِر
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۲۶۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3407
> *English:* "A land whose plain is mountain, whose water is a trickle, whose dates are the poorest kind, whose enemy is a champion …" ʿUmar said: "Are you making rhymes at me, or reporting?"
**عبرت:** A frontier was closed because one officer was made to say plainly what he had actually seen.
**Hands-up?** *The empire is winning everywhere. Where does it stop?*

---

### GSA/E-ST38 · سلمان ؓ, dying, on the provisions of a rider
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ⚠ **NOT in this window** — under عثمان ؓ, ۳۳–۳۶ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** المدائن
**What happened:** سعد بن أبي وقاص ؓ and ابن مسعود ؓ came to see سلمان ؓ at al-Madāʾin as he
lay dying, and found him weeping. It was not fear of death and not love of the world. It was one
instruction of the Prophet ﷺ that he thought he had not kept. Then he turned to سعد ؓ, the
conqueror of Iraq, and gave him three clauses. When he died he left twenty-odd dirhams.
**The statement:**
> عَهْدٌ عَهِدَهُ إِلَيْنَا رَسُوْلُ اللهِ - صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ - لَمْ نَحْفَظْهُ. قَالَ:
> (لِيَكُنْ بَلاَغُ أَحَدِكُم مِنَ الدُّنْيَا كَزَادِ الرَّاكِبِ) . وَأَمَّا أَنْتَ يَا سَعْدُ، فَاتَّقِ
> اللهَ فِي حُكْمِكَ إِذَا حَكَمْتَ، وَفِي قَسْمِكَ إِذَا قَسَمْتَ، وَعِنْدَ هَمِّكَ إِذَا هَمَمْت
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء ج۱ ص۵۵۲ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1978
> *English:* "A charge the Messenger of God ﷺ laid on us which we have not kept: 'Let what any one of you takes from this world be like the provisions of a rider.' And as for you, Saʿd — fear God in your judgement when you judge, and in your dividing when you divide, and in your intention when you set your mind on something."
**عبرت:** A man who left twenty dirhams thought he had taken too much.
**Hands-up?** no. **Say plainly that this is later than tonight's twelve years** — see §5.

---

### GSA/E-ST39 · «Sāriya — the mountain!»
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۲۳ھ (فَسَا / دَارَابجَرْد) `[SOURCED — ابن كثير: «إسنادٌ جيدٌ حسنٌ»]` · **Map:** فارس — فَسَا and دَارَابجَرْد
**What happened:** سارية بن زنيم ؓ was being surrounded far away in Persia. In Medina, in the
middle of the Friday sermon, عمر ؓ broke off and called out a name and two words, three times.
Nobody in the mosque understood it. When the army's messenger arrived he said they had been breaking
when they heard a caller, put their backs to the mountain, and were saved. ابن كثير gives four
routes for this, grades one of them good, and says they strengthen one another. And عمر ؓ, asked
what it had been, refused to claim anything for it.
**The statement:**
> يا ساري الجبل (يا ساري الجبل … فقيل لعمر بن الخطاب: ما ذلك الكلام؟ فقال: والله ما ألقيت له إلَّا بشيء
> ألْقِيَ على لساني
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۷ ص۲۶۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3406
> *English:* "Sāriya — the mountain! Sāriya — the mountain!" … ʿUmar was asked: "What was that speech?" He said: "By God, I gave no thought to it at all — it was something cast onto my tongue."
**عبرت:** The man it happened through took no credit for it, and that is the part worth keeping.
**Hands-up?** *He stopped in the middle of the khuṭba and shouted a name. Why?*
**⚠** Narrate it exactly as ابن كثير does, grading and all, and put عمر ؓ's own line on the same slide. Do not build anything on it.

---

### GSA/E-ST40 · معاوية ؓ's three-line verdict on the two caliphs
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** later; a retrospective judgement `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** n/a
**What happened:** معاوية بن أبي سفيان ؓ, looking back, summed up his two predecessors and then
his own generation in one sentence.
⚠ **This card has no §1–§6 entry behind it; it stands on its page alone.** The three-clause wording
is سیر's (راشدون ص۸۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1226). البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۶۴–۲۶۵ carries the same
saying but the sentence breaks across the page and **البدایہ's copy has only the first two clauses
in that wording** — «أمَّا أبو بكر فلم يُرد الدنيا ولم تُرده، وأما عمر فأرادته فلم يردها». Quote سیر.
**The statement:**
> أما أبو بكر فلم يرد الدنيا ولم ترده، وأما عمر فأرادته الدنيا ولم يردها، وأما نحن فتمرغنا فيها ظهرا لبطن
> — سیر (سير الخلفاء الراشدين) ص۸۱ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1226 · also البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۶۴–۲۶۵
> *English:* "As for Abū Bakr — he did not want the world and it did not want him. As for ʿUmar — the world wanted him and he did not want it. As for us — we rolled about in it, back and front."
**عبرت:** The clearest description of the change came from a man describing himself in it.
**Hands-up?** no.
**⚠** This is a bridge into session 4 and it must be left as a bridge. **Read it, and stop.** Do not
gloss it, do not extend it, and do not let it become an argument about anyone.

---

### GSA/E-ST41 · الذہبی رحمہ اللہ corrects his own book
**Tier:** GOOD (for «کیسے پتا چلا؟», not for narrative) · **When:** written ~8th c. AH about سلمان ؓ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** n/a
**What happened:** People said سلمان ؓ had lived three hundred and fifty years, and that two
hundred and fifty of them were beyond doubt. الذہبی رحمہ اللہ had himself written the figure of
two hundred and fifty into his own earlier history. On this page he takes it back — in public, in
writing — reasons from what سلمان ؓ actually did with his life, puts him in his eighties, and then
invites correction from anyone who knows better.
**The statement:**
> فَمَنْ كَانَ عِنْدَهُ عِلْمٌ فَلْيُفِدْنَا … وَقَدْ ذَكَرْتُ فِي (تَارِيْخِي الكَبِيْرِ) أَنَّهُ
> عَاشَ مَائتَيْنِ وَخَمْسِيْنَ سَنَةً، وَأَنَا السَّاعَةَ لاَ أَرْتَضِي ذَلِكَ، وَلاَ أُصَحِّحُه
> — سیر أعلام النبلاء ج۱ ص۵۵۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1982
> *English:* "So whoever has knowledge of it, let him inform us … I stated in my *Tārīkh al-Kabīr* that he lived two hundred and fifty years, and at this moment I do not accept that and do not hold it sound."
> ⚠ **Both sentences are on ص۵۵۶, and they run in this order on the page** — the invitation to correct
> him first, the retraction a few lines later. The 350/250 figures they answer are on ص۵۵۵
> (https://shamela.ws/book/10906/1981).
**عبرت:** The men who preserved this history corrected themselves in writing, and left the correction in the book.
**Hands-up?** *If a historian finds he was wrong twenty years ago, what does he do?*

---

### GSA/E-ST42 · CUT — recorded so it is never re-researched
**Tier:** CUT · **When:** various · **Map:** n/a
**What happened:** Five items were read, verified and deliberately set aside. They are listed here with their pages so
that a future pass does not spend a second on them:
1. **The long homiletic passage «أين الجبّارونَ … أين الملوكُ»** after أبو بكر ؓ's second address —
   البدایہ ج۷ ص۹. Two printed pages, and سيف's transmission.
2. **The messenger who ate with عمر ؓ without knowing him** — البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۵۹, ۲۶۲. Fine as
   narrative; **no quotable statement**, and the only dialogue in it is domestic.
3. **أم كلثوم ؓ's exchange with عمر ؓ about her clothing** — البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۵۹. Domestic; leave out.
4. **The commanders' brocade tunics at الجابية and the pebbles** — البدایہ ج۷ ص۱۵۸. One line of colour
   in the script at most; not a slide.
5. **خالد ؓ and the silk shirt, and the audit of his wealth** — سیر ج۱ ص۳۸۰–۳۸۱. The audit report is
   **FLAGGED WEAK (سيف)** and the silk-shirt exchange needs a fiqh footnote about the concession
   granted to ابن عوف ؓ, which is more than the evening can carry.
**The statement:** none in the sources we use.
**عبرت:** —
**Hands-up?** no.

---

## ابن خلدون: how he reads it (framing only)

*Source note: [`ibn-khaldun-on-the-ridda-the-conquests-and-method.md`](../docs/research/ibn-khaldun-on-the-ridda-the-conquests-and-method.md) — read it for the pages behind these cards.*

### IKO/E-IKM1 · The count is the weakest part of the story
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a — a claim about method, not a dated event · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn opens his book with a warning, and it is not about lying. It is about
counting. He says that when historians report sums of money and the sizes of armies, that is exactly
where a report is likeliest to be false. He does not say the battles did not happen. He says the
numbers travelled differently from the events. His reason is simple. Nobody was ever going to check.

**The statement:**
> ولا سيّما في إحصاء الأعداد من الأموال والعساكر إذا عرضت في الحكايات إذ هي مظنّة الكذب ومطيّة الهذر
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۳ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/11
> *English:* "— and especially in the counting of sums of money and of armies when they appear in the
> narratives, for these are the place where falsehood is to be expected and the mount that idle talk
> rides."

**عبرت:** A number nobody could check is the part of a story that travels least well.
**Hands-up?** Yes — *"When a report says an army was a hundred thousand men, who counted them?"*

---

### IKO/E-IKM2 · Two figures for one army, in one book
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn warned that army-counts are the likeliest thing in a history to be
wrong. Then he wrote his own history. On page fourteen he records the Persian host at al-Qādisiyya
twice: a hundred and twenty thousand on one transmission, sixty thousand on another. He does not
choose between them. A hundred and eighty-four pages later, arguing a different point, he uses the
larger figure. The man is not being careless. He is showing us, in his own hand, exactly what he told us to
expect.

**The statement:**
> وأعظم ما كانت جموعهم بالقادسيّة مائة وعشرين ألفا كلّهم متبوع على ما نقله سيف … وعن عائشة والزّهريّ
> فإنّ جموع رستم الّذين زحف بهم سعد بالقادسيّة إنّما كانوا ستّين ألفا كلّهم متبوع
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/12
> *English:* "The largest their host ever was at al-Qādisiyya was one hundred and twenty thousand,
> every one of them a man with followers, according to what Sayf transmitted … And from ʿĀʾisha ؓ and
> al-Zuhrī: the host of Rustam against which Saʿd ؓ advanced at al-Qādisiyya was only sixty thousand,
> every one of them a man with followers."

**عبرت:** The honest historian is not the one whose numbers agree; he is the one who shows you that
they do not.
**Hands-up?** No. Put ج۱ ص۱۴ and ج۱ ص۱۹۸ side by side and let the room read them.

---

### IKO/E-IKM3 · Seven ways a report goes wrong, and partisanship is the first
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn does not treat falsehood in history as the moral failure of individual
liars. He treats it as a thing with causes, and he lists them. Seven. The first is partisanship for an
opinion or a school. His point is unsettling and exact. It is not that a partisan lies. It is that a
partisan stops checking, because the report already agrees with him. The scrutiny he would give
anything else never gets given at all.

**The statement:**
> وإذا خامرها تشيّع لرأي أو نحلة قبلت ما يوافقها من الأخبار لأوّل وهلة وكان ذلك الميل والتّشيّع غطاء
> على عين بصيرتها عن الانتقاد والتّمحيص
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۴۶ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/44
> *English:* "When partisanship for some opinion or creed has got into it, it accepts whatever agrees
> with it at first hearing — and that inclination and partisanship become a covering over the eye of
> its insight, keeping it from criticism and scrutiny."

**عبرت:** The report you are least able to test is the one that already agrees with you.
**Hands-up?** No. This one lands better read plainly than asked about.

---

### IKO/E-IKM4 · A fiqh test and a history test are not the same test
**Tier:** CORE — **this is the guardrail** · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn draws a line that this whole course rests on, and he draws it in one
sentence. Checking the chain of transmitters, he says, is the right test for a legal report — because
a legal report imposes an obligation, and an obligation must be secure before it binds. But a report
about an event needs something else first. It needs to be possible. If a thing could not have
happened, examining who narrated it is wasted effort.

**The statement:**
> وإنّما كان التّعديل والتّجريح هو المعتبر في صحّة الأخبار الشّرعيّة لأنّ معظمها تكاليف إنشائيّة
> أوجب الشّارع العمل بها … وأما الأخبار عن الواقعات فلا بدّ في صدقها وصحّتها من اعتبار المطابقة
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۴۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/47
> *English:* "Vetting the transmitters is the operative test for the soundness of legal reports,
> because most of them are prescriptive obligations the Lawgiver has made binding … But as for
> reports about events, their truth and soundness require testing them against correspondence."

**عبرت:** A report of an event is neither a ruling nor a creed; it is a record, and it is weighed as
one.
**Hands-up?** No. Deliver it flat and move on.

---

### IKO/E-IKM5 · The disease that only shows itself after centuries
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn names an error he calls hard to catch, and he is right. It is the habit
of reading a later century's meanings back into an earlier one. He calls it a deep-seated disease,
extremely well hidden, because it only becomes visible after long stretches of time have passed. A man
is not wrong about the fact. He is wrong about what the fact meant to the people it happened to.

**The statement:**
> ومن الغلط الخفيّ في التّاريخ الذّهول عن تبدّل الأحوال في الأمم والأجيال بتبدّل الأعصار ومرور
> الأيّام وهو داء دوي شديد الخفاء
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۳۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/35
> *English:* "Among the hidden errors in history is inattention to the fact that the conditions of
> peoples and generations change as the ages change and the days pass. It is a deep-seated disease,
> extremely hard to detect."

**عبرت:** Distance in time is not only distance in dates; the words themselves have moved.
**Hands-up?** Yes — *"If someone in the year 2500 read that a man taught school, what would they
assume about him?"*

---

### IKO/E-IKM6 · The schoolteacher's son
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** the point concerns الحجاج بن يوسف, d. ۹۵ھ `[STANDARD]` (to verify) ·
**Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn gives an example of that error, and it is a small, clean one. People
read that al-Ḥajjāj's father taught the Qurʾān and conclude the family were nobodies. He answers that
in the first age of Islam teaching was not what it is in our own day, a trade to live by.
Al-Ḥajjāj's father was one of the chiefs and nobles of Thaqīf. The reader had the fact right and the
century wrong.

**The statement:**
> والحجّاج بن يوسف كان أبوه من سادات ثقيف وأشرافهم … ولم يكن تعليمه للقرآن على ما هو الأمر عليه لهذا
> العهد من أنّه حرفة للمعاش
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۴۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/38
> *English:* "As for al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf, his father was among the chiefs and nobles of Thaqīf … and
> his teaching of the Qurʾān was not what the matter is in our own day, that it is a trade for a
> living."

**عبرت:** The same word does not carry the same weight in two different centuries.
**Hands-up?** No.

---

### IKO/E-IKM7 · He tests the method on a figure nobody is attached to
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn wanted to show how to test a number, so he picked one nobody in his
audience had a stake in. Historians reported six hundred thousand fighting men counted in the
wilderness. He asked four questions. Could that land feed them. Could a field hold them. Did any
empire we know of ever put such a number on a battlefield. And could one family line reach that size
in four generations. The answers ruled the figure out. He never had to accuse anyone of lying.

**The statement:**
> والحاضر يشهد لذلك فالماضي أشبه بالآتي من الماء بالماء.
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/12
> *English:* "And the present bears witness to this — for the past resembles what is to come as water
> resembles water."

**عبرت:** What you can see today is the only measuring stick you have for what you cannot.
**Hands-up?** Yes — *"How would you check a number nobody alive ever counted?"*

---

### IKO/E-IKM8 · He calls al-Bukhārī a historian
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** A room that trusts the Ṣaḥīḥ absolutely can still be wary of the word history. Ibn
Khaldūn closes that gap in one clause. He says the ancients held history in the highest regard, and
then names the men who took it up. Al-Ṭabarī. Al-Bukhārī. And Ibn Isḥāq before both of them. These are
not three kinds of men. They are scholars of the umma working one discipline with different aims.

**The statement:**
> وما استكبر القدماء علم التّاريخ إلّا لذلك حتّى انتحله الطّبريّ والبخاريّ وابن إسحاق من قبلهما
> وأمثالهم من علماء الأمّة
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۳۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/35
> *English:* "The ancients held the science of history in such high regard only for this reason —
> so much so that al-Ṭabarī and al-Bukhārī, and Ibn Isḥāq before them, and their like among the
> scholars of the umma, took it up."

**عبرت:** History was never outside the scholarship of this umma; it was one of its instruments.
**Hands-up?** No.

---

### IKO/E-IKA1 · What actually holds a people together
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a — but this is the concept the ۱۱ھ tribal map rests on

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn gives kinship a name and a job. He does not call it a virtue and he
does not call it a vice. He says the bond of kin is simply natural in people, and that part of that
bond is the surge of feeling when a relative is wronged. That surge is what makes a group defensible.
He anchors it on the brothers of Yūsuf, peace be upon him, saying that nobody would dare touch a man
who has a kin-group behind him.

**The statement:**
> وذلك أنّ صلة الرّحم طبيعيّ في البشر إلّا في الأقلّ ومن صلتها النّعرة على ذوي القربى وأهل الأرحام
> أن ينالهم ضيم أو تصيبهم هلكة
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۶۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/158
> *English:* "For the bond of kinship is natural in human beings, save in the fewest of cases; and
> part of that bond is the surge of feeling on behalf of close relatives and kinsfolk, that no wrong
> should reach them and no ruin befall them."

**عبرت:** Before there were states in Arabia there were kin, and kin were the only protection anybody
had.
**Hands-up?** Yes — *"In a land with no police and no courts, who protects you?"*
⚠ Say the two senses of عصبية out loud before this card (§4.1), or half the room hears the condemned
sense.

---

### IKO/E-IKA2 · The man standing alone slips away in the dark
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn explains what happens to a man with no kin behind him. Not in peace —
in battle. He writes that a man alone in his lineage feels no surge on anyone's behalf, and nobody
feels one on his. So when the air darkens with evil on the day of fighting, each such man slips away
looking to save himself. It is not an accusation of cowardice. It is an observation that there was
nothing holding him there.

**The statement:**
> وأمّا المتفرّدون في أنسابهم فقلّ أن تصيب أحدا منهم نعرة على صاحبه فإذا أظلم الجوّ بالشّرّ يوم
> الحرب تسلّل كلّ واحد منهم يبغي النّجاة لنفسه
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۶۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/158
> *English:* "As for men standing alone in their lineage, seldom does any of them feel that surge on
> his comrade's behalf; so when the air darkens with evil on the day of battle, each of them slips
> away seeking his own escape."

**عبرت:** Men hold a line for the people beside them before they hold it for anything else.
**Hands-up?** No.

---

### IKO/E-IKA3 · Nothing gets carried without a people to carry it
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn takes the point about defending a camp and widens it as far as it will
go. Anything that has to be carried against resistance, he says, needs a group behind it. He names
three things in one breath: prophethood, the founding of a realm, and a call. Human beings resist;
resistance has to be met; and meeting it takes people who will stand together. He states it as a rule
with no exceptions.

**The statement:**
> فبمثله يتبيّن لك في كلّ أمر يحمل النّاس عليه من نبوة أو إقامة ملك أو دعوة إذ بلوغ الغرض من ذلك
> كلّه إنّما يتمّ بالقتال عليه لما في طبائع البشر من الاستعصاء ولا بدّ في القتال من العصبيّة
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۶۰ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/158
> *English:* "The like becomes clear to you in every undertaking men are called to — prophethood, the
> founding of a realm, or a summons — since attaining the aim in any of these is only completed by
> fighting for it, given what is in human nature of resistance; and in fighting there is no doing
> without عصبية."

**عبرت:** A truth still needs people willing to stand for it before it moves anything in the world.
**Hands-up?** No.

---

### IKO/E-IKA4 · Leadership does not leave the line it started in
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn makes a claim about how leadership moves inside a kin-group, and it
explains the shape of a great many arguments in early Arabia. Leadership, he says, sits in one branch
of a stock, not in the whole of it, and it passes only to the strongest branch of that same stock. An
outsider, however long attached and however trusted, does not acquire it. He is an attached man, and
attachment gives no ascendancy.

**The statement:**
> والسّاقط في نسبهم بالجملة لا تكون له عصبيّة فيهم بالنّسب إنّما هو ملصق لزيق وغاية التّعصّب له
> بالولاء والحلف وذلك لا يوجب له غلبا عليهم البتّة
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۶۵ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/163
> *English:* "One who has no standing at all in their lineage has no عصبية among them by descent; he
> is merely an attached, adhering outsider, and the most that can be felt for him is by clientage or
> alliance — and that gives him no ascendancy over them whatsoever."

**عبرت:** In a world organised by kin, the first question about any leader was not what he had done
but whose he was.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ **HANDLING — this card explains the *shape* of the argument at سقيفة, never its merits.** Ibn
Khaldūn himself never applies it there (§7.3). Do not let it drift into adjudication.

---

### IKO/E-IKA5 · Where kinship-strength ends up
**Tier:** GOOD — **for session 4, not session 1** · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn does not leave group strength as a description. He gives it a
destination. It runs, he says, towards one end, and that end is kingship. Leadership is being
followed; kingship is being obeyed. And a man who has reached the first and can see the road to the
second does not stop, because the human soul wants it. He states this as a natural tendency, not as a
judgement on any particular man.

**The statement:**
> فالتّغلّب الملكيّ غاية للعصبيّة كما رأيت
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۷۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/172
> *English:* "Royal ascendancy is the end-point towards which عصبية runs, as you have seen."

**عبرت:** Group strength has a direction of its own, and it does not stop where its holders intended.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ **This is the seed of the خلافت → ملوکیت theme. `CLAUDE.md` §1.5 flags session 4 as one of the two
evenings that carry real charge with this organisation. Deploy it there, attributed to him, and not
in session 1.**

---

### IKO/E-IKA6 · Many small loyalties, or one big one
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn describes what happens inside a single tribe that contains several
houses. Each house has its own loyalty. Unless one of them is strong enough to draw the rest after it
until they fuse into a single great loyalty, the tribe splits, and the splitting leads to disagreement
and then to strife. It is the plainest possible account of why a confederation is either led or is
nothing.

**The statement:**
> فلا بدّ من عصبيّة تكون أقوى من جميعها تغلبها وتستتبعها وتلتحم جميع العصبيّات فيها وتصير كأنّها
> عصبيّة واحدة كبرى وإلّا وقع الافتراق المفضي إلى الاختلاف والتّنازع
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۷۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/172
> *English:* "There must be one عصبية stronger than them all, which overcomes them and draws them
> after it, so that all the عصبيات fuse into it and become as if one single great عصبية — otherwise
> there follows the fragmentation that leads on to disagreement and strife."

**عبرت:** A gathering of loyalties either becomes one loyalty or becomes a quarrel.
**Hands-up?** No.

---

### IKO/E-IKR1 · ⭐ The call does not add numbers; it removes rivalry
**Tier:** CORE — **the headline slide of this strand** · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn asks the question anyone looking at the map of the year eleven would
ask. How does a peninsula that has just fragmented move against two empires within two years? His
answer does not multiply anyone's numbers. He says a religious call takes away the rivalry and the
mutual envy that exist among people bound by kin, and points them all one way. And once that happens
nothing stands in front of them — because the direction is one, the object is equal in every eye, and
they will die for it. He names the conquests as his own example.

**The statement:**
> والسّبب في ذلك … أنّ الصّبغة الدّينيّة تذهب بالتنافس والتّحاسد الّذي في أهل العصبيّة وتفرد الوجهة
> إلى الحقّ فإذا حصل لهم الاستبصار في أمرهم لم يقف لهم شيء لأنّ الوجهة واحدة والمطلوب متساو عندهم
> وهم مستميتون عليه
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۹۸ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/196
> *English:* "The reason for it … is that the religious colouring takes away the rivalry and mutual
> envy that are in people of عصبية, and makes their direction single, towards the truth. Then, once
> clear sight in their affair is theirs, nothing stands in their way — because the direction is one,
> the object is equal in all their eyes, and they will die for it."

**عبرت:** What changed in Arabia was not how many men there were, but which way they were all facing.
**Hands-up?** Yes — *"Two years after the tribes broke apart, they were moving on two empires. What
had changed?"*
⚠ **The slide must carry his name and the words "as he reads it".** He never wrote this about the
ridda; joining his chapter to the year eleven is our doing (§7.2).

---

### IKO/E-IKR2 · The verse he grounds it on
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn does not leave the mechanism as a piece of social observation. He puts
a verse under it. Kingship comes by ascendancy, he says, and ascendancy comes by group strength — but
the gathering and joining of hearts comes only by a help from Allah in establishing His religion. And
then he quotes al-Anfāl: had you spent everything in the earth, you could not have joined their
hearts.

**The statement:**
> وجمع القلوب وتأليفها إنّما يكون بمعونة من الله في إقامة دينه قال تعالى «لَوْ أَنْفَقْتَ ما في
> الْأَرْضِ جَمِيعاً ما أَلَّفْتَ بَيْنَ قُلُوبِهِمْ»
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۹۷ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/195
> *English:* "The gathering of hearts and their joining comes only by a help from Allah in the
> establishment of His religion. He said, exalted be He: 'Had you spent all that is in the earth, you
> could not have joined their hearts.'"

**عبرت:** The joining of those hearts was never something anyone could have bought.
**Hands-up?** No.
✅ **The safest possible carrier for the whole analytical point in this room** — it puts the cause
where the audience already puts it.
⚠ The Qurʾānic line goes on the slide in `Traditional Arabic` Naskh and must be eyeballed against a
muṣḥaf (`CLAUDE.md` §1.3). The edition's inline «٨: ٦٣» reference is dropped when the verse is set on
a slide; give the reference in the caption instead.

---

### IKO/E-IKR3 · The test that runs the other way
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn does not stop at the claim. He supplies the condition under which it
would fail, which is what makes it an argument and not a slogan. Watch, he says, what happens when the
religious colouring alters and decays. The whole thing unravels, and the outcome falls back to the
measure of bare group strength, without the increment religion had been adding. The same peoples, the
same numbers, a different result.

**The statement:**
> واعتبر ذلك إذا حالت صبغة الدّين وفسدت كيف ينتقض الأمر ويصير الغلب على نسبة العصبيّة وحدها دون
> زيادة الدّين
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/197
> *English:* "And take note of this: when the colouring of religion alters and decays, how the whole
> matter unravels, and ascendancy reverts to the measure of عصبية alone, without the increment of
> religion."

**عبرت:** The increment was never owned; it was only ever held while it was being lived.
**Hands-up?** No.

---

### IKO/E-IKR4 · Why it had to be a religious call and could not have been a political one
**Tier:** CORE · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn explains why the peninsula could not have been unified on any other
basis. The Arabs, he writes, were the hardest of peoples to make yield one to another — from
harshness, from pride, from far-reaching ambition, and above all from rivalry over leadership. Their
inclinations, he says, seldom converge. Only when religion came did the restraint come from inside
them instead of being imposed from outside, and then yielding and gathering became easy. And he adds
at once that they were also the quickest of peoples to accept guidance, because their natures were
sound.

**The statement:**
> فقلّما تجتمع أهواؤهم فإذا كان الدّين بالنّبوءة أو الولاية كان الوازع لهم من أنفسهم وذهب خلق الكبر
> والمنافسة منهم فسهل انقيادهم واجتماعهم
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۸۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/187
> *English:* "Their inclinations seldom converge. But when religion comes by prophethood or by
> sanctity, the restrainer over them is from within themselves, and the trait of arrogance and
> rivalry departs from them, so their yielding and their coming together become easy."

**عبرت:** A restraint a man carries inside himself holds where one imposed from outside does not.
**Hands-up?** Yes — *"What would it have taken to make these tribes obey one man?"*
⚠ **Always pair it with the sentence on the same page** — «وهم مع ذلك أسرع النّاس قبولا للحقّ
والهدى» — or the first half reads as contempt for the Arabs and the room will bristle.

---

### IKO/E-IKR5 · A call with no people behind it
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn writes the other half of his own law, and it is the half that keeps him
honest. A religious call without a group behind it, he says, does not come to completion. He means it
structurally, not spiritually: a summons still has to be carried by people who will stand together for
it, and where there is no such body, the summons does not travel. He gives a worked example from his
own Andalus.

**The statement:**
> [الفصل السادس في أن الدعوة الدينية من غير عصبية لا تتم]
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۱ ص۱۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/197
> *English:* "Chapter Six: that a religious call without عصبية does not come to completion."

**عبرت:** A call still needs a community willing to stand under it.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ On that page he cites a ḥadīth and calls it ṣaḥīḥ. **We have not verified it. Use the chapter's
claim, never the ḥadīth (§7.4).**

---

### IKO/E-IKN1 · Whole tribes, parts of tribes, and those who simply held off
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۱۱ھ `[SOURCED]` for the statement; البدایہ carries the events ·
**Map:** the ridda map must show three states, not two

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn opens his account of the year eleven with a distinction any honest map
of it needs. The Arabs turned back, he writes — either the whole tribe entire, or a part of it. And a
page later, listing the fronts, he records a third state as well. Ṭayyiʾ and Asad gathered around
Ṭulayḥa; Ghaṭafān turned back; and Hawāzin simply held off, withholding the ṣadaqa. Three states,
not two.

**The statement:**
> وقد ارتدت [١] العرب إمّا القبيلة مستوعبة وإمّا بعض منها
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۲ ص۴۸۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/1286
> *English:* "The Arabs had turned back — either the whole tribe entire, or a part of it."

**عبرت:** A map drawn in two colours will always be telling you less than the sources did.
**Hands-up?** No.
✅ Corroborates `arabian-tribes-and-the-ridda-setup.md` §3.5 (بنو تميم split three ways, البدایہ
ج۷ ص۲۸) from a **second, independent author**. Worth saying out loud.

---

### IKO/E-IKN2 · The sheep in the rain — and who really said it
**Tier:** GOOD · **When:** ۱۱ھ `[SOURCED]` · **Map:** n/a

**What happened:** There is a famous sentence about the state of the Muslims when the Prophet ﷺ died:
that they became like sheep out in the rain on a winter's night. Ibn Khaldūn uses it. It is not his.
Ibn Kathīr carries it twice, once from Ibn Isḥāq and once through a second chain, and Ibn Isḥāq is
the older source by centuries. This is worth saying aloud, because it shows the audience exactly how
a phrase travels, and why the citation has to follow it back.

**The statement:**
> وصارَ المُسْلِمون كالغنم المَطيرةِ في اللَّيلة الشّاتية، لفقدِ نَبيّهم ﷺ، حتى جَمَعَهم اللَّهُ على
> أبي بكر ﵁.
> — البدایہ والنہایہ ج۵ ص۳۹۷، عن ابن إسحاق · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/2581
> *English:* "And the Muslims became like sheep out in the rain on a winter's night, for the loss of
> their Prophet ﷺ — until Allah gathered them upon Abū Bakr ؓ."

**عبرت:** A sentence worth repeating is worth tracing back to the man who first said it.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ **Cite البدایہ ج۵ ص۳۹۷, never ابن خلدون ج۲ ص۴۸۹.** This card is itself a small demonstration of §1's
boundary.

---

### IKO/E-IKN3 · One dissent does not unmake a consensus — and even that is said conditionally
**Tier:** GOOD, **with the handling note obeyed exactly** · **When:** ۱۱ھ `[SOURCED]` — the year is
page-cited in `saqifah-bani-saida.md` (البدایہ ج۵ ص۳۴۳؛ ج۷ ص۵), not on ابن خلدون's page ·
**Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn's account of the pledge to Abū Bakr ؓ ends with a short constitutional
remark rather than a story. The Muhājirūn and the Anṣār were of one mind, he says; one man dissented,
and no notice was taken of it because it stood alone. What is worth noticing is the clause he puts in
the middle. Before drawing any conclusion at all, he writes: if his dissent is even established. He
will not build on a report he has not first weighed.

**The statement:**
> أجمع المهاجرون والأنصار على بيعة أبي بكر ولم يخالف إلا سعد إن صحّ خلافه
> — تاریخ ابن خلدون ج۲ ص۴۸۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/12320/1286
> *English:* "The Muhājirūn and the Anṣār were of one mind on the pledge to Abū Bakr ؓ, and none
> dissented but Saʿd ؓ — if his dissent is even established."

**عبرت:** A careful historian states his condition before he states his conclusion.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ **HANDLING, and it is not optional.** Quote **only** as far as «إن صحّ خلافه». The words that follow
(«فلم يلتفت إليه لشذوذه») **do not go on a slide and do not go beside سعد بن عبادة ؓ's name.** Ibn
Khaldūn also repeats on that page the verse claiming the jinn killed him: **that is exactly the sort
of report §2 teaches us to reject, and it is never repeated.** If pressed from the floor, answer from
البدایہ via `saqifah-bani-saida.md` and defer, per `CLAUDE.md` §1.6.

---

### IKO/E-IKX1 · The chapters that must be read whole before they are quoted at all
**Tier:** CUT — recorded so it is never re-researched · **When:** n/a · **Map:** n/a

**What it is:** Two chapters of the Muqaddima are routinely quoted against the Arabs:
«[الفصل الخامس والعشرون في أن العرب لا يتغلبون إلا على البسائط]» (ج۱ ص۱۸۶) and
«[الفصل السادس والعشرون في أن العرب إذا تغلبوا على أوطان أسرع إليها الخراب]» (ج۱ ص۱۸۷). They are on
the page and they say what they say. **They are not used in this series, for three reasons, all
recorded here so the decision is not revisited:**

1. **He is describing the البدو الوحشيون — nomads outside any settled order — not the Muslims of the
   فتوحات.** The Shamela edition's own footnote at ج۱ ص۱۸۱ says so: «يعني ابن خلدون العرب المتوحشون».
2. **He reverses the whole judgement two chapters later.** At ج۱ ص۱۹۰ he says their nature is
   overturned and changed by a religious colouring, and that under the Sharīʿa «عظم حينئذ ملكهم وقوي
   سلطانهم». Quoting the first without the second misrepresents him.
3. **The room.** These chapters cannot be delivered in forty-five minutes without either sounding like
   an attack or requiring a defence that costs the evening its subject.

**The statement:** none used. The passages are at ج۱ ص۱۸۶ (https://shamela.ws/book/12320/184) and
ج۱ ص۱۸۷ (https://shamela.ws/book/12320/185); the reversal is at ج۱ ص۱۹۰
(https://shamela.ws/book/12320/188).

**عبرت:** A judgement quoted without the qualification its author attached to it is no longer his
judgement.
**Hands-up?** No — and if it is raised from the floor, the answer is reason 2 above, with the page
number.

---

### IKO/E-IKX2 · Four good sentences — one of them usable after all
**Tier:** CUT — recorded so the corroboration is done once, cheaply · **When:** ۱۴–۲۳ھ `[STANDARD]`
(to verify) — an era tag, not a page-cited date; only «وفي هذه السنة وهي أربع عشرة» for بناء الكوفة
والبصرة is on the page (ج۲ ص۵۴۹) · **Map:** n/a

**What it is:** Ibn Khaldūn's narrative records four explanations that would each make a fine slide.
Al-Hurmuzān telling ʿUmar ؓ that Persia will keep fighting while its king remains among them
(ج۲ ص۵۵۳). Saʿd ؓ explaining that Arab troops sicken in the Iraqi lowlands, which is why al-Kūfa and
al-Baṣra were built at all (ج۲ ص۵۴۹). ʿUmar ؓ's building rule for the new cities, ending "hold to the
sunna and the state will hold to you" (ج۲ ص۵۵۰). And ʿUmar ؓ's mobilisation address (ج۱ ص۱۸۱).

**All four are sayings, and `DECISIONS.md` #29 forbids him as the authority for the wording of a
saying — so each had to be found in an approved work or dropped. Re-searched across the entire cached
البدایہ، سیر and الکامل with harakat stripped (an earlier bare-consonant grep returned a false
negative), the result is one out of four:**

> ✅ **ʿUmar's ؓ building rule IS in الکامل ج۲ ص۳۵۴, almost verbatim, in the same context — so it is
> usable, cited to الکامل and never to Ibn Khaldūn.** "Hold to the sunna, and the state will hold to
> you" comes off the CUT list.
>
> > فَقَالَ: افْعَلُوا وَلَا يَزِيدَنَّ أَحَدُكُمْ عَلَى ثَلَاثَةِ أَبْيَاتٍ، وَلَا تَطَاوَلُوا فِي
> > الْبُنْيَانِ، وَالْزَمُوا السُّنَّةَ تَلْزَمْكُمُ الدَّوْلَةُ.
> > — الکامل في التاريخ ج۲ ص۳۵۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1045
> > *English:* "Do so; and let none of you exceed three rooms; and do not build high; and hold to the
> > sunna, and the state will hold to you."

The other three stay out. Al-Hurmuzān's advice and ʿUmar's ؓ mobilisation address are in no approved
work. Saʿd's ؓ answer is only half there: الکامل ج۲ ص۳۵۲ has "the unwholesomeness of the country
changed us" **in the delegation's mouth, not his**, and no camel clause at all — so the *reason* the
two cities were founded may be narrated from الکامل, but **the sentence may not be put in Saʿd's ؓ
mouth**. They are recorded in §6.6–§6.7 with their pages. Until corroborated: **absent, not wrong**.

**عبرت:** A sentence you cannot yet stand behind is better left out than said and defended.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ Set the vowelled الکامل text on any slide, and eyeball it against a page image first
(`CLAUDE.md` §1.1).

---

### IKO/E-IKX3 · Rustam watching the prayer ranks
**Tier:** CUT — recorded so it is never re-researched · **When:** ۱۴–۱۵ھ `[STANDARD]` (to verify) —
the conventional date of القادسیہ; **no date at all appears on ج۱ ص۱۹۰, which is analysis** ·
**Map:** n/a

**What it is:** At ج۱ ص۱۹۰, immediately after arguing that religion put the restraint inside the
Arabs, Ibn Khaldūn records that Rustam, seeing the Muslims forming up for prayer, would say that
ʿUmar ؓ had eaten his liver — that he was teaching manners to dogs. As an observation it is powerful:
the enemy commander recognising that what he faces is discipline rather than numbers.

**It is not used, and the decisive reason is not the one you would guess: in the approved works the
line belongs to somebody else.** "ʿUmar has eaten my liver" is **Abū Luʾluʾa's**, wept over the
captives of Nihāwand — البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۳۴، الکامل ج۲ ص۳۹۹ — **and Ibn Khaldūn himself gives it to
Abū Luʾluʾa in volume 2, at ج۲ ص۵۵۸.** Only "teaching manners to dogs" is peculiar to ج۱ ص۱۹۰, and it
appears on no other page in any cached tree. Add to that: it is a saying, and `DECISIONS.md` #29
forbids him as the authority for a wording; and «الكلاب» is contempt for the Muslims that will land
badly however it is framed.

**The statement:** none in the sources we use. The Rustam version is at ج۱ ص۱۹۰ ·
https://shamela.ws/book/12320/188; the Abū Luʾluʾa version is at ج۲ ص۵۵۸ ·
https://shamela.ws/book/12320/1355, البدایہ ج۷ ص۲۳۴ · https://shamela.ws/book/30097/3380, and
الکامل ج۲ ص۳۹۹ · https://shamela.ws/book/21712/1090.

**عبرت:** What an enemy noticed about a people is worth knowing — but only once you know who reported
it.
**Hands-up?** No.
⚠ **This is a third internal contradiction inside his own book (cf. §3.4), and the cleanest possible
illustration of why `DECISIONS.md` #29 bars him from carrying a wording. It is not usable as a
story, but it is usable as the reason for the rule.**

---

### IKO/E-IKX4 · The judgement that is not in the book
**Tier:** CORE — **as the honesty line of whichever slide carries his name** · **When:** n/a ·
**Map:** n/a

**What happened:** Ibn Khaldūn is the great explainer of Islamic history, and it is tempting to say
that he explained the ridda. He did not. Ninety-five pages of his narrative of the years eleven to
twenty-three were read for this session, and searched for every phrase he uses elsewhere when he is
about to explain something rather than report it. There were two. His account of these years is
narration. The analysis is four hundred pages away in another volume, and it never once mentions the
ridda. Putting the two together is our work, and the audience is told so.

**The statement:** none in the sources we use — **and that is the finding.**

**عبرت:** Saying plainly where a book stops is part of using it honestly.
**Hands-up?** No — but this sentence, or one like it, belongs on any slide in this series that carries
his name.
